THIS investigation was undertaken primarily to consider the possibilitr of Iiomologiziny the sulci of tlie cerebral hcmisplicre in different Orders of Nammals. With this object in view, thc brain mas cszlmiiied in almost every genus in the whole class of Mammalia ;

but as this yielded such an immcnse aiid unwieldy mass of data, it became necessary, both for the sake of clcarness and conciseness of exposition as vvell as to admit of the adequate discimion of the significance of tlic facts, to limit the scope of the enquiry, or, rather, to select tlie most instriwtive body of evidence which might typify the conclusions to be drawn from a study of tlie whole mass of data.

In the first place, attention has been almost n~liollyc oncentrated on the consideration of the homologies of tlie furrows called ‘‘calearinc ’)and ‘‘ Sj-lvian.” Tiicse are the only sulci (in addition, of course, to the hippocampal and rliinal fissures) which are absolutely constant in all Primates. At the outset of this iiivcstigation it became abundantly evident;, firstly, that if the calcarine sulcus is a distinctive feature oE the Primates and is not represented iii the brain of other Marnmalia, the search for liomologues of tlie other, less stable, furrow on the mesial surface of tlic lieniisphcre must dfortiori be utterly futile ; and, secondly, if the Sylviun fissure of tlie Apes and Man is represented in other mammals b,v 110 sulcus other than that inconstant and cxceedingly unstahle series of difewnt * f‘iirrows,which in various Carnivores, Ungulates, Edentatcs, Rodents, aiid Marsupials is commonly called “Sylvian,” tlic possibility of homologizing any sulcus on tlie lateral aspect of the hemisphere of the Primates with a furrow on thc surface of the brain of other mammals becomes utterly destroyed. That this not altogether obvious conclusion must inevitably be drawn from tlie preniiss just stated will be abundantly shown in the Eollowing pages. We shall, therefore, best arrive at sonic decision regarding the main question, by devoting our attention chiefly to the Sylvian and calcarine furrows, without, however, altogether neglecting the other sulci. I n the second place, I have deemed it desirable to further limit the dimensions of’this work, by restricting the detailed account

This process of curtailiiient has been aecomplidied in two wajs.

of the sulci (and tlic organ in which lliey occiir) to one circuniscribed group of mammals ; and, so as not to also narrow the scope of this discussion by so doing, I have freely introduced coinparatiye data, which serve to indicate the behaviour of each individual sulcus in the whole 3Ianimalian class.

Tlie brain of the Leniurs has been selected as the aptest expression of these requirenicnts. I can best explain this by anticipating some of the conclusions of this research. The features of tlie l’rosiniian brain 1)ecome really intelligible only on the supposition that the Lemurs haye advanced i~ considerable distance in the main stream of the civolution of the Primates and have then retrograded ; among other manifestations of this retrogressive process inany interesting phases of the disintegration of the cerebral sulci are exhibited, so that it becomes possible to recognize the constituent elements of inany compound sulci in the l’rimafes, and sn the more readily to compare them with the fnrrows found in other mammals.

My choice of the Lemurs was further confirmed by other considerations of a very different nature. The opportunity of cxamining a much more representative collection of Prosimian brains Ilian liad previously fallen to the lot of any one observer, and the unique facilities for studying the brain-form of the extinct Lcmuroids, for which I ain indebted to the kindness of Dr. Forsyth Major, were additional reasons for choosing the 1)rainof the Lemurs as the touchstone on which to test tlie hypotheses suggested by ii study of the brain Moreover, the present state of our knowledge of the mammalian lwain is so unsatisfactory, that no apology would be necessary for discussing its apparently obvious features in any Order, and least of all iu tlie enigmatical family of Lemurs; for in no group of mammals is accurate knowledge of the brain more urgently wanted than in the latter. Tlie voluminous literature of the l’rosimisn h ain is particularly barren so far as exact and authentic statements of fact are concerned, and unduly rich in the grossest inaccuracics.

other mammals.

The foregoing statement of the oliject of these notes will explain their nature and scope. They do not pretend to be a monograph on the Prosimian brain, but are ratlier an account of the more variable features of the mammalian brain, as these are exhibited in the Lemurs. I have deemed it advisable, if for no other purpose than to give some idea of the ‘‘setting ” of the pattern of the cerebral convolutions, to give a brief account of the outstanding features of the brain as a whole. The morpliology of tlie inammalian cerebellum is incidentally discussed also.

Jn attempting to settle such an essentially morphological problem as the search after a fundamental plan underlying the apparently heterogeneous maze of cerebral sulci found in the different Orders, the observer is afforded an excellent opportunity of appreciating at their true worth the factors wliicli disguise the common plan. Tlicb value of the evidence of the brain as an indication of the closeness of the bonds of’ affinity between tlie Lemui s and other ms~mmalshence falls not unuaturally within the scope of this study.

THE LITEltATURE *.

Since Tiedemann t. first described the brain of Lemur 92~011902,a rcry considerable number of memoirs on tlie Prosimian brain liave been publislied. The interest of tlie numerous early descriptions, which include tliose of Leuret (1S391, Scliroeder ran der Kolk and Vrolik (1846-51), and van dcr IIocven and van Cainpw (lS59),is almost purely historical. But the same period yielded an escellent memoir by Burmeister on tlie brain of Tarsius$, which represents to the present day all the accu~ateinfornmtion we possess concerning this brain. It is of further interest as tlie first really valuable contribution to our knowledge of the Prosimian bwin.

The most critically-careful descriptions of any Lemurs’ brains are coiitained in the

two memoirs of W. H. Flower 0,which include brief accounts of the brain in Lenim*

$%laus [)?ig?*ifrons]and Nycticebus tcti*d&radus, with notes on that of a Gtrlugo.

I n 18SG Richard Owen published the first account of the brain of C%iromp,in a memoir 11 which lacks the clearness and definiteness of Flower’s monographs.

In 1S72 Paul Gervais gave an interesting account of the shape of thc brain in tlie various genera of Lemurs from the data supplied by the study of cranial casts ; but as

lie made little or no attempt** to check the results of his examination of tlie cranial

moulds by comparison with the actual brains, he draws many inferences concerning tlie latter, and also with regard to the affinities o i the Leniurs (see the titleof his memoir !), which are not justified by a study of the brain itself.

In 18’75A. Milne-Edwards gave an account of tlie h ain in tlie Indrisinztt, wliiclt represents all the published data concerning tlie actual brains in tlie genera 3zrZris, Propithecus, and Avakis. [Many recent writers (such as Flutau and Jticobsohn, whose acquaintance witli the bibliography of their subject is as slight as their knowledge of the actual mammalian brain) attribute the knowledge of the brain in 1iid1-i~to Chudzioski, who borrowed from Milne-Edwards’s monograph, and to Zuckerkancil, ivho makesthe merestreferenceto the brain of YropitAecusdiucleinu$$.I

Dr. Porsyth Major, whose generous help has been afforded nie in many ways during the course of this inyestigation, gave me the brain of Microcebus Sinithi and lent me the skulls of various Lemurs. On his suggestion, the authorities in the Geological and Zoological Departments of the British Museum kindly permitted ine to examine tlieir large collections of crania of recent and extinct Lemurs. I have also examined all the crauia of Prosirniz in the collection at the ltoyal College of Surgeons.

I have thus been able to study the shape of tlie brain and the arraiigeiiient of the cerebral furrows not only in every genus and most species of recent Lemurs, but also in the extinct Lemw Jullyi, Globileiiaw, and both an adult and a young Xe~aladapis..

*is Dr. Forsyth 3Iajor was engaged in investi3ztinp these valuable extinct forms, my indebtedness to him is all the greater for allowing me to examine them.

It is commonly supposed that a mould of the cranial cavity can give little exltct information beyond indicating the shape of the brain. In niost ,4pes this is to a very great extent true. But in most niainnials in which the pattern formed by the sulci is not yery coniples (and also even in some, especially Carnivores, in which a very rich s:ipply of sulci exists) a series of prominent ridges develop on the inner face of tlie cranial wall so a9 to accurately map out tlie plan of the sulci. These ridges are especially well developed in all Lemurs, excepting only the extinct form Globilemw, in which the pattern becomes blurred as in most Apes.

It is, however, ahsolutely necessary, if one is to accurately interpret the plan of the sulci from the arrangement of these bony ridges, to carefully study the actual brains of several genera and the crania in which they were lodged, to appreciate several sources of fallacy, which have been so unfortunately demonstrated in Gervais’suncontrolled study of casts (op.cit. siqmr). If this precaution be taken, one is able to map out the plan of the siilci on the outer surface of the henlispliere in a plaster mould with the certain conviction that he is accurately interpretiug the configuration of tlie brain itself.

Proni the specimens (Lenzw,Ngcticebus, -7lic1*ocebus,and Tursizrs)which I have been able to devote to liistological purposes, I have been able to determine many interesting features in the arrangement of the fibre-tracts, especially the optic and olfactory paths

To Dr. Henry Woodward, Dr. Charles W. _Indrews,ancl Mr. Oldfield Thomas, I am indebted for the opportunity of examining the large collectious of mammalian crania in tlit: British Museum. Mr. W.L. H. Duckworth, in Cambridge, also freely permitted me to make use of the considerable collection of mainnitlliaiz brains in his possession.

For duable gifts of material for coinparatiw purposes, I am under the greatest obligations to Professor Howes, the late Jlr. Xartin Woodward, Professors Wilson niid Baldwin Spencer and Mr. James 1’. Hill (_imtralia),Dr. ltobert Brown (South -ifrica), Dr. Charles Hose (Borneo).and especially Captain Stanley Flower (Egypt), aniong many others. Without the rich (ancl I believe unprecedented) collection of representative mammalian brains which I have thus been able to study, this communication would have lost its only claim to consideration, i. e. that it truly sets forth the tendencies of ?)rain-evolutionin the whole 3~animalia,and not only in one siiiall circuiiiscribed group.

For the facilities which havc been so generously afforded me in the accomplishiiient of this work I must express niy great sense of indebtedness to all the gentlenien who have assisted me.

###### TJIECEREBRALHEMISPHEREIS THE GENUSLEXR.

It is not necessary to describe the genera1 forin qf the brain in the various generi oc Lemuroidea, because the bulk of the literature quoted above deals with this subject, autl certain of them, such as that of Gervais, are concerned exclusively witli the shape of the brain. Xoreover, all the important features of the brain-form will be discussed incidentally in these notes along with those peculiarities which are causally related to the modifications in shape.

When the dorsal surface of the brain of a Lewzir is exposed by removing the roof of the cranium, little else than the cerebral hemispheres can be seen. In front of the pointed anterior pole of each hemisphere, the exposed area of tlie olhctory bulb projects sery slightly more than one millimetre : and behind the broader, somen-liat spatulate caudal extremities of tlie hemispheres in most cases only a very narrow band of the cerebellum is exposed as a series of seven rounded knobs fringing the margins of the hemispheres (fig. 1). So that in order to study the relationships of tlie constituent parts of the brain, it is necessary to turn to the examination of the ventral surface (fig. 4). In front there is the broad flattened olfactory bulb, about 11inm. long aud

about half as broad, lying in contact with tlie under surface of the cerelwal hemisphere

except in the anterior 1.5nxn. of its extent *.

Its posterior end is joined to a flattened peduncle wliich is as broad as the bulb itself. It is very short, for it pursues a course of only 3 or E mm. before it becomes attached to the base of the cereliral hemisphere. In this respect it differs most decidedly from the very long ancl attenuated olfactory peduncle of the Apes ;but, on tlic other hand, a short thick peduncle like that of the Lemur is found in the vast majority of mammals, and must be regarded as the common mammalian type.

The lateral half of the peduncle is covered by a dense thick bundle oE fibres wliicli constitutes the tractus olfactorius [lateralis]. This white covering also extends over the lateral border of the peduncle. The internal half of the ventral surface and tlie mesial border present a greyish appearance, because the medullary fibres of the so-called mesid tract are much less numerous and more scilttered than those of the liltera1 tntct. I n the elongated peduncle of all Monkeys the wliole peduncle appears to consist of nerve-fibres, so that its real constitution is not so obvious as it is in the Lemurs.

Just behind the insertion of tlie olfactory peduncle into the base of the hemisphere there is a transversely-placed, oval, puckered area of grey substance, 4 mm. broad and about 2.5 mm. in the sagittal dii-ection. This is thc tuberculuiii olfactoriumn peculiar cortical formation coating the ventral surface of the head of the corpus striatum. The features of this 1)oclyare exceedingly constant throu;liout the Marnm&t, hut 1)ecome somewhat obscured in tlie adult brain of Man and the Antliropoid Apes. The lnesial surface of the olfwtoq peduncle extends on to the corresponding surface of the hemisphere just in front of the tuberculurn olfactorium.

The lateral wall of the olfActory peduncle is prolonged backward into continuity \vitIi the pyriform lobe. The latter begins in front as a iiarrow horizontally pluced grey band 3 inin. wide, the internal part of which is covered by the (cxterId) olfactory tract. Opposite the outer extremity of the tuberculum olfactorium it becomes bent at right :ingles upon itself (fiq. 6) and at the same time broadens to almost four times the width, SO that its posterior pirt forms a very prominent rounded boss projwting 1. iiitn. the level of tlie ventral surface of the anterior part. The transverse depression Jornled by this siidclen bend is the vallecula Sylvii-a furrow which is quitra distinct from the fossa and fissura Sylvii.

!he vallecula Sylvii is much deeper and more pronounced in the 1, m i i r than in most manirr;alian brains of other orders. I n some of the smaller New-N'orld -Ipes, such as the Hapalidae, the vallecula presents features exactly analogous to that just descrilied, the pohterior pait of the pyriforni lobe being bent at an mglc of !I0 on the anterior part ; but in most Apes the angle of bending becomes much more acute, the posterioipart bulges forward over the anterior part of the pyriform lobe ; ancl the vallecula Sylvii becomes a very deep cleft, mliicli is cominonly regiirded as part of tlic stciii of tlw true S~lvianfissure.

[Figure 11]

3IORPHOLOGY OF THE BRAIX IN THE MdJIJIAL1.A. ,329

At the antero-mesial aspect of the natiform eminence (as the posterior part of the pyriform lobe is often called) there is an elliptical area hounded latei-ally by a shallow groove. Retzius lias called this tlie "gyms lunaris " and has shown how remarkably constant it is in the Jfmrmalia *. I am not aware that anyone has ever considered thct significance of this littJe body (which I have studied not only in most Mammals, but also in the Reptilia) ; its nature is at once revealed in the Lemur's brain by a section at right angles to its loug axis. It is clearly nothing else than the surface of the nucleus arnygdals and is not therefore strictly a part of'the pyriform lobe.

At the mesial border of the p3riform lobe we find a peculiar depressed grey band, w-hich is erroneously labelled "ggrus uncinatus " by FIatau and Jacobsohn+. It is merely tlie lowcr end of tlie fascia, dentata, whereas the uncinate gyrus is represented by tlie pyriform lobe itself. These writers adopt a very unusual and utterly misleading nomenclaturc when they call the anterior part of the pyriform lobe by the name"gyrus liippocaiiipi" (p. 105).

In most, Lemurs the rhinal fissure, wliicli is tlie lateral boundary of the pyriform lobe, becomes almost wliollp obliterated. This also happens in such Cliiroptera as Ptei-opus and C'ynoizyclcris. I have, however, been fortunate enough to obtain the brain of R Lemwfztlurts, in w11Ich the rliinal fissure is retained in a form as distinct as it is in most mammals (figs. 4 and 6). I lrave also in my possession another brain of the same genus in which the whole of the rliinal fissure is visible, altliougli it is very shallow ; and also the cranial cast of get another in wliicli it is quite distinct. These three examples arc, however, exceptional.

The fissure is distinctlr visible upon the lateral aspcct of the first-nientioned Itrain (fig. 6) pursuing a course (from the cleft between the olfactory bulb and the hemisphere) hackward with a very slight upward inclination. Just behind the vallecula Sylvii it suddenly bends downward at an obtuse angle and curves horizontally backward to a point midway between the rallccula Sylvii and the posterior margin of the hemisphere. Behind this point there is no line of demarcation between thc pyriform lobe and the neopallium.

In Monkeys no part of the rhinal fissure is visible upon the lateral aspect of the hemisphere: the relatively greater size of the neopallium and the much smaller absolute dimensions of the pyriform lobe shift it wholly on to the ventral, or even mesial, surface.

In the brain of most members of the genus Lemw, all that remains to represent tlie rhinal fissure is the angle and often only the posterior limb of the angle (fig. 7). In such cases, however, a careful examination enahles one to distinguish the pyriform lobe fronz tlie neopalliurn, and thus determine the situation from which the fissure has been ohliterated.

'theolfactory tract (fig. 4)ends in the rallecula Sylsii in a distinct " tubercle of the

The liippocampal formation is thus partly exposed as a series of hands ascending

[Figure 13]

MORPHOLOGP OF THE BRAIS 1pu’ THE NAJIJIALIA. 331

approximately vertically from the patch of fascia dentata on the ventral surface to the posterior end, or splenium, of the corpus cnllosum.

In the greater part of its extent this hippocampal formation conforms to the plan which we may call characteristically mammalian (such as we find, for instance, in the brain of Tamundua [z;ide the “ Brain in the Edentata,” op. czt. 8upru, figs. 10 & 23, p. 317)]. There is, as in Tumunduu,a well-defined sub-splenial flexure, and the wrinkled band of fascia dentata curves around on the under surEace oE the corpus callosum in the characteristic manner and then tapers as it merges in the circumcallosal hippocampal vestiges. A little hemispherical area of ‘‘hippocampus nudus ” fills up the concavity of the arc formed by the sub-splenial bending of the fascia dentata,just as I have represented it in Funzundua (op. cit. p. 320, fig. 23). In all of these respects the hippocampal formation conforms to the typical mammalian condition, which I have described in detail in the above-quoted work.

In a recent memoir Zuckerkandl has briefly referred to the “ hippocampus nudus” (his “Balkenwindung ”) in the Lemurs *. He describes it as being ‘‘zapfenformige ” in I;emw cuttu, L. ouri~~8,L. mongoz, and L.. coyozatm, a.nd ‘(Icume zapfenformige ’’ in Chirmys.

The lower extremity of the hippocampal formation presents a peculiar modification not found in most mammals. Several years ago I called attention to the peculiar fact that a part of the hippocampus is rolled in such a manner that it becomes completely invert.ed: the surface which was originally deep is then superficial, and vice verstc (Journ. of Anat. and Phys. vol. xxxii. p. 28). A strip of this “inverted hippocampus ” separates the fascia dentah from the fimbria in the Lemur, as also happens in most mammals; but this becomes greatly expanded at its lower extremity to form a ‘‘hippocampal tubercle,” such as is found in Oryctei*opzcs(“ The Brain in the Edentata,” op. cit. fig.23, p. 315). A hippocampal tubercle of similar constitution occurs in all the -4pes, and has been accurately described in the human brain by Giacomini and Retzius (compare Journ. Anat. and Phys. 1-01. xxxii. pp. 28 S: 29).

In the Prosimian brain the tubercle vat-iesconsiderably in size and prominence, and in the brain of Nycticebzc8 practically vanishes; it is never so prominent as it is in the Apes, although its constitution is the same. In some Insectivores, such as Nucroscelidm, I have seen a condition closely resembling that of the Lemurs, and have also seen instances of the same phenomenon in Orycteropzts and in the brain of a Manatee. This peculiar condition is apparently inherited by all the Primates, both Lemurs and Apes, from some primitive Insectivore-like ancestor, which has also transmitted it to the primitive Aard-vark and that peculiar Eocene stock which has persisted with little change as the Sirenia.

The corpus callosum is very long and has a very plump caudal extremity or splenium, and a very thick and fully-recurved genu. As a whole, it closely resembles that of the Platyrhine Apes. The greatly attenuated psalterium, or hippocampal commissure, resembles the common mammalian type, such as is found, for example, in

ILSr~~za~~diiu,Felis, or Ocis. It is, hon-ever, hardly so plump as in any of these mammals, and yet it is not nearly so siiiall and at,tcnuated as that of the Spes. In the Hnpalidze, for example, the psalterium becoines so estreinelj~reduced (vide Journ. of

- h a t . and l’hys. vol. xxxii. fig. 23, p. 61) that Beevor failel to find it even 1)y Iiistologicnl means. In tlic Lemurs its size is more considerable, and t81ie chief accumulation of its fibres is never wholly separated from the splenium by an interval devoid of commissural fibres,as happens in Midas (or).cif.)and the other Hapalid:*.

The septum lucidum, precommissural area, and anterior conimissure present the usual relations.

Imniediately behind the hippocampal tubercle there is a very deep indentation, which limy possibly represent the caudal rcninant of tlie rhinal fissure (fig. ;,a). It begins close to (hut without actually joining) the hippocampal fissure and passes obliquely downward and backward. On thc base of the brain (fig. 4)it has all the appearance of joining tlie hippocampal fissure. Its extent and direction vary considerably in different 1)rains of even the same species. In many cases (as, for instance, fig.5 ) its dorsal lip projects downward as a prominent ronndcd knob overlapping the deepened notch.

Upon the mesial surface of the hemisphere there are a nunib:lr of \-cry stablc and constant sdei, and nsuallp some otlier more variable furrows.

By far the most important of tliese is a qroup of three, which may 1~ called the calcarine complex. This is a t r idiate pcztterii of sulci situated behind the splenium of the corpus callosum ; the ventral limb may lie distinguished as “c:th~ine,”the caudal as “rctrocalcarine,” and tlie dorsal as “ paracalcarine.”

Rllove and parallel to tlie corpus callosum there is a sulcus which will be called “inter-

calary”; and in front of the geiiu there is generally a “genual sulcus”* of Kruep

#### (fig. a).

There is always an ohlique sulcus placed below the retrocalcarine sulcus, which for the present may be referred to simply as 6.

There is also a very oblique sulcus (c)on the caudal margin of the hemispliere, midway lwtween the furrows CI and b.

That portion of tlie mesial surface of the cerebral Iicmisphere wliich lies lielow and 1,ehind the corpus callosuni is adapted to the shape of the contiguous structures. A deep depression receives the optic thalami, a much smaller and shallower depression below and Iwliind the splenium is produccd by the quadrigeminal bodies, and the broad flattened area behind it is so moulded 11:- contact with the upper surface of the cerebelhm.

The calcarine sulcus begins inferiorly within the deep thalamic fossa just above the sulcus n ; it ascends for about 3 mm. parallel to the hippocampal fissure and then 1)endingbackward it crosses the rim of the thalamic fossa, traverses the mesencephalic f‘ossa,and ends in the cerebellar arm, about 3 nim. behind the splenium. It attains a dt@h of G mm. in its posterior part.

In every brain (uitli 0111~-one exception) belonging to the genus Lemur which I have examined the calcarine sulcus was continuous with the retrocalcarine sulcus, tile two

* It is not altogether certain whether it may not rel)reseiit the ‘‘rostral” sulcus.

furrows uniting at an angle of 120’. The retrocalcarine sulcus is confined to the cerebellar area. It passes backward with a very slight upward inclination for a distance of R min. In some cases it is almost horizontal; in others it is removed as much as 30” from that plane. -It its meeting place with the cnlcarine sulcus it is as deep as the latter.

The degree of obliquity of this sulcus shows considerable variability.

The paracalcarine sulcus passes oldiquely upward and forward from the angle of junction of the otlier two sulci almost as far as (in rare cases even actually crossing) the dorsal margin. I n some cases its terminal piece is bent vertically upward. This snlcus is generally shallower than the calcarine, so that it appears to cut deeply into the npper lips of (without actunlly joining) the sulcus formed by the junction of the calcarine atid the retrocalcarine furrows. In one case, however, I have seen the calcxrine and paracalcarine siilci joined, the retrocalcarine being tlie separate element. This is instructive, because it showsthut the retrocalcarine snlcus possesses a certain amount of individuality and is not a mere mechanically-produced extension of tlie calcarine sulcus. The sulcus b varies considerably in Icngth and depth in different brains. In the large brain of n /,eii~iirctr~iusI haw seen it represented by a very faintly marked depression less than

In otiier cases it is a furrow S mm. long and 2 or even 3 mm. deep.

- 3 mm. lony. It, undoubtedly represents the most caudal element of that composite sulcus of tlic human brain known as the ‘‘collateral.”

It may be a mere pit in the depression caused by the roof of the bony capsule of the floccular appendage (so-called ‘(petrosnl ” lobule) of the cerebellum, or it may become a deep sulcus 5 or 6 nim. long.

The sulcus c is even more variable.

On the external aspect of thc neopallium the sulci are no less variable (figs. 6 & 7). The deepest and most constant furrow is that which is commonly called tlic Svlviim fissure. It really consists of an uppweut blending of two nctriul?~sepnmte overlapping sulci. One of these, which I shall call pseudosylvian, proceeds vertically upward (for :L distance ot’ 4 mm.) from the bend (or more strictly from the posterior limb) of the rhinal fissure. It appews to (but does not actually) join a second sulcus, which is obliquely placed, so that tlie two sulci form an angle of about 150”. This upper oblique sulcus I shall call the ‘(suprasylvian.”

If the lips of these sulci be separated, it mill be found that thc sulcua pseuclosylvius cuts deeply backward into the heinisphere, so that its posterior lip forms an operculum (4mm. long and almost equally thick) which overlaps a depressed area (fig. 6 a). Thr lower part of the snlcus suprasylvius overlaps the s. pseudosylvius (fig.G), and the antcrior lip of tliis part of the furrow is also slightly operculated. Hence there is ;in area (about G mni. long and 1 mm. high) submerged and hidden from view by tlic anterior opercular lip of the suprasylvian and the posterior opercular lip of the pscudosylvian sulci (fig.6 u). The few observers who have in any way referred to this depressed area regard it as the insula Keilii. Holl (op. cit. szqwa) regards it as the submerged homologue of Leuret’s first srcuate gyrus of tlie Carnivora. The degree of accttracy of these statements will be discussed below.

Between the upper end of the suprasylvian sulcus and the dorso-mesial edge of the liemisphere there is a sagittal sulcus of very varialile length, which may be distinguished

[Figure 16]

by the name The anterior extremity of this sulcus always bends laterally, so as to be approximately parallel to the upper part of the suprasylvian sulcus (fig. 1). Its posterior extremity usually becomes bent in a mesial direction. It often happens, however, tlist this mesially directed limb remains as a transverse sulcus widely separated from the sulcus lateralis (fig. 3). It may be distinguished as the postlateral sulcus.

lateral.’’

Behind and parallel to the suprasylvian and pseudosylvian sulci there is a sulcus of varying length and depth, which may be distinguished as postsylvian.

The mode of termination of the supra- and postsylvian sulci is exceedingly variable (compare figs. 1,2, 3, 6, and 7). In many cases the upper extremity of the suprasvlvian sulcus bends backward so as to almost reach the postsylvian (fig. 6): in other cases (fig. 7) the latter curves forward to approach the suprasylvian ; and in other cases again (tig. 3) the intercalated furrow is separated from both sulci. We may distinguish it by the letter d, and defer the discussion of its significance until later.

From a point about 5 mm. in front of the middle of the suprasylvian sulcus a furrow (which may attain a length of 20 mm.) passes obliquely forward and inward to end upon the anterior pole of the hemisphere. For reasons which will be discussed later, I shall distinguish this sulcus by the not-obviously appropriate name ‘‘coronalis.” In almost all cases the posterior part of this furrow is bent backward and inward toward the lateral sulcus (fig. 2). We may distinguish this little fragment from the rest of the sulcus by the letter e.

Midway between tliis sulcus and the mesial border there is a very short furrow, the direction of which is by no means constant. In most cases, however, it is directed laterally and forward.

For the present I shall call it the “sulcusf.” It is the orbital sulcus,

Upon the orbihl surface of the hemisphere there is a small sulcus of very variable size, depth, and shape alongside the olfactory bulb. In some brains it is either quite absent or represented only by a very shallow depression.

Immediately in front of the lower extremity of the suprasylvian sulcus (which is generally exposed in the genus Xenrzw) tlicre is often a small oblique sulcus in the orbital margin.

This I shall call ‘‘diagonal.”

Near the floccular notch (in the caudal margin of the hemisphere) there is generally a small oblique sulcus, which may or may not extend into the floccular depression and overlap the sulcus c.

It may be distinguished for the present by the letter g.

[In the foregoing enumeration of these sulci I have deliberately used terms which no other writer has employed in reference to the Lemur’s brain: not only to avoid the hopeless confusion which the varied nomencluture of Beddard, Ziehen, Chudzinski, and Flatau 3rd Jacobsohn would entail, but also for morphological reasons, which will appear later and, I believe, justify this course of action.]

These measurements are extraordinarily large when it is considered that the brain weighed only 26.3 grms., after being in 10 per cent. formol solution for eight days.

The corresponding measurements in an adult female L. mcccaco I found to be 1.3mm., 37 mm., and 26 mm. respectively. These measurements (which were checked hy comparison with the cranium) indicate a brain-shape very different to that of Flatau and Jaeobsohn's specimen. If the latter mere swollen as the result of the formalin, the value of the elaborate tables oE measurements in their book is seriously discounted.

The hemispheres of :I LC~ZIII*ficTuus were 46 mm. long and 38 mm. broad ;and tlie corresponding measurements in a Lemur cnritis were 49 mm. and 43 mm. respectively.,

The size of the brain of thc extinct Lemzcr JuZZyi greatly exceeded that of any of the recent Lemurs; for its ccreliral hemispheres were 59 mm. long, 48 mm. broad, and 31 mm. deep.

Turning to the consideration of the cerebral sulci and fissures, a considerable amount of variation is found.

The rliinal fissure usually becomes aborted, with the exception of a very small furrow near the olfactory peduncle and the cq~pco~ei~trecurved inferior extremity of the pseudosylrian sulcus. In tlie specimen of Lenzui?fiiZ2:m described above, the greater part of the fissure persisted. In another specimen of 5.fzdaus, and in one each of L. ~nacctco itnd A.V Q ~ ~ UIS have, seen a faintly marked furrow representing the whole fissure.

The two brains of A.vai-iris (one in ttie College of Surgeons and one from Ghizeli) agree in the following respects :-The diagonal sulcus is absent ; the orbital sulcus is so poorly developed as to be a mere insignificant depression ; the lateral sulcus is a simple regular arc, widely separated from the postlateral and coronal sulci ; the sulcus cl is not joined to either the supra- or to the postsylvian sulcus ; the collateral sulcus is very small and shallow ; and, apart from the small floceular sulcus, there is no sulcus behind the Iiostsylvian.

All of these features may be regarded as indicative of a tendency to simplicity, which is all the more significant in that the brain in this species is appreciahly larger than that of most other members of the genus. It remains for future research to determine wliether tliese features may be regarcled as distinctive of the species vccrirts.

I have examined two brains of Leti~ur?IACGCOCOa~idsm-eral cranial czsts. In additiou Flatau and Jacobsohn (op.cit.) have given a full description of a brain ofthis species.

The diagonal and orbital sulci are constantly present, but the latter shows a tendency to become irregular by the derelopment of a latcral ramus. Sometimes also its direction varies. The mode of ending of the upper extremities of the supra- and postsylvian sulci is variable. In most cases tlic upper estreinity of the suprasylvian sulcus is simple ; but Flatau and Jacolisohn represent (Taf. 3. fig. 1, 4) a small added horizontal dcus. Generally the upper end of tlic parallel sillcus curves forward, representing a fusion with the sulcus cl, which usually does not exist as a separate element in this species. Unlike the condition found in L. vii)"is, there is no distinct postlateral sulcus, but there is a very oblique furrow, united at a very obtuse angle with the ctluclal end of the lateral sulcus.

I have never seen the sulcus described as '' tempoiwlis secundus (oberer Ted) " by

[Figure 19]

MORPHOLOGY OF THE BRAIS IK THE MABlMALIA. 337

Platau and Jacobsohn in tlie brain of Lemur cai*ius: nor have I el-er found the sulcus laldled "Nebenfurche " (Taf. 3. fig. 2, 9 ) in the brain of any Lemur.

In the brain of Lemui*$clws the diagonal sulcus is generally well-developed and often deeply notches the supraorbital margin.

The lateral and coronal sulci approach much more closely than is the case in the other species, and in some instances they even seem to join. 'L'hus Beddard says (Proc. 2001. SOC.1895,p. 143):-" In the brain of Lemur ntoiiyoz the an,nular [lateral-1 and inferofrontal [coronal] sulci are completely continuous." But even if this sometimes happens, it is by no means the usual condition in L. ficlaus. For, as the same writer remarks (p. 143),this does not occur in L ~~lbifi.02~~,nor ill L.i)~igiif?~oi~snor L. nnjuunensis,all of which may be included in the speciesf&m. Within this species all varieties of the upper ends of the supra- and postsylvian sulci are found. Sometimes a long arcuate sulcus cl is fused at one end to the suprasylvian sulcus and almost joins tlie postsylvian at thc other. Not infrequently the sulcus (I is joined to the postsylvian and not to the suprasylvian ; but in some cases it remains independent of both, as in the species curiics.

In most cases the caudal end of the lateral sulcus behaves as it does in tlie species i)i~ciccico,and not as in rcii*ius.

In the two specimens of Lenzzcr cattu wliicli I examined there was nothing distinctivci and they might well have belonged t,othe species fulcus.

The skull of Lemur rubriuenter, Geoff., exhibits ridges corresponding to typical suprasylvian, postsylvian, lateral, postlateral, coronal, orbital, and diagonal sulci, with nothing indicative of a distinct specific type.

The large brain of the extinct Lemur Jzrllyi (Pnlcz?c?hognleusJidlyi, G. Grand.) exhibits the typical Lemurid plan of sulci with diagrammatic clearness.

The suprasylvian sulcus with a long deeply-incised inferior exposed limb, theshallower furrow indicating the pseudosylvian sulcus ; simple, long, linear orbital and diagonal sulci; lateral and coronal sulci which almost (if not quite) meet as in Lei)mwfztZ2;u.s; and a small sulcusf, all conform to the most typical Leniurid pattern.

The orbital sulcus presents none of the irrezularity which it has acquird in most of tlic recent Lemiirs.

###### THEBRAININ THE LORISIX-E.

It is conrenicnt to consider A>cticebus first. The brain in this genus is not only

'l'he cerebral hemispheres of the only fresh specimen of n'yclicebzis tcridi,p*atZ?uwhich sudler Clian that of Lemur, but also presents some noteworthy differences in shape. I have seen are 34 mm. long, 2s mm. Ride, 23 mm. high.

- 340 PROF. G. ELLIOT SMlTH OX THE

If a vertical line be drawn from the most proniinent part of the ‘‘natiform eminence ” of the ppriform lobe, it will be found that more than half of the hemisphere lies behind the vertical line.

This is not tlie case in Lenzw, but is so in all Apes.

There is a more pronounced caudal extension of the hemisphere, so that the cerebellum is almost completely overlapped.

There is a mnch smaller “ orbital area ” in Nycticebus than in Lemur. In the latter it extends in the caudal direction as far RS tlie pseudosylvian sulcus, whereas in the former it only extends to a point midway between the pseudosylvian sulcus and the anterior pole of thc hemisphere.

In the qenus Lemzw the olfactory bull, is a Iiorizontally flattened plate, the bulk of which is covered Iiy 1he Ilcmisphere. In the genus Xycticebas the bulb is a vertical sagittal plate, 5 nini. of tlie length (9 mni.) of wliicli projects Iw;vond the anterior pole of the hemisphere.

I t is oiily 1.5 mni. I~road(i.e. transverse thickness).

The anterior rhinal fissure is not obliterated : of the posterior rhinal fissure only one or two millimetres exist as a furrow, but if’ a fresh I)rain be examined a 1-ery faint depression is visil)le extending horizontally backwaid with a slight downward bend. This can be traced on to the nicsial surface.

The flexure of the pyriform lobe is slightly more acute ant1 pitliecoid than that of k m w ,so that the rallecula Sylvii is more cleft-likc. The other parts of the rhinencephalon closely resemble the corresponding regions in the genus Lenzzw, IF it11 the cxception of the lower end of the hippocampus. For in Xyclicebtcs, unlike every other l’rosimian known to me, there is no well-defined liippocanipal tubercle. The inverted hippocarnpus merely becomes slightly broader at its lower end, as it does in most mammals (fig. 12).

Fig.12.

SL.\ sp‘‘.

fh. Jc.

A:qcticebtu tardipadus. Mesial aspect of the right cerebral hemisphere. Kat. size.

The corpus callosum is a very thin plate of fibres 12 mm. long; its anterior and posterior parts are not so markedly thickened as they are in Lemur. There is an exceptionally extensive arcuate genu.

The form and relations of the calcarine group of sulci are identical with those described in Lemw. The paracalcarine sulcus is, however, much shorter. The intercalary sulcus is relatively larger than it is in Lenzzcr, and its posterior extremity is slightly bifid : the upper limb being the deeper faintly foreshadows the upturned posterior end of the calloso-marginal sulcus of the Anthropoidea. There is neither a genual nor a rostra1 sulcus.

[Figure 23]

###### NUKPHOLOOY OF THE BRAIN IN THE X4;\1;\1dLIA. 341

The Sylvian fissure is composed of two separate sulci, pseudosylvian and suprasylvian, as in Lemw; but in this genus they both pursue the same direction and overlap so slightly that it requires the closest examination to detect the lomer end of the suprasylvian sulcus. As the result there is no obvious depressed area, such as is found in Lemzcr, but its liomologue is found in the anterior lip of the furrow in the region of the lower end of the suprasylvian sulcus. The lower part of the pseudosylvian sulcus is very shallow.

The upper extremity of the suprasylvian sulcus becomes directly continuous with the lateral sulcus, no submerged gjms being found to separ*ate the two

elcments.

The lateral sulcus bends backnwd, and then finally curves inward iu a manner exactly analogous to that of Lemurfihics.

Tfierc is a small bracket-shaped sulcusft tlie concavity of whichfacesthe middle line.

There is a sulcus about 7 mm. long in front of and parallel to the suprasylvian sulcus. Thus the sulcus may be regarded as consisting of two limbs .c and y. There is always a small sulcus just above the orbital inargin wliich we may regard as the sulcus coronalis. In my type specimen this is so small as to be little else than a mere pit.

Its upper part is bent backward so as to become almost horizontal.

There is a simple linear postsplvian sulcus.

In my specimen (fig. 10) there are no other sulci, nor even depressions to represent tlie sulci called a, b, c, and 9 in the brain of Lenau1‘.

In the specimen of Nycticebzis tavdipaclzcs [Stetzops javnnicus, Illig.] described by Flower (Trans. 2001. SOC. vol. v. pp. 103 et scq.) there are some slight differences. Thus the sulcus y appears to be absent and z is joined to the coronal sulcus, the resultant furrow being called “hfero-frontal,” which is the name Flower gives to the coronal sulcus of Lenzw. The sulcusfis represented as a long sagittal furrow, which is called (‘supero-frontal” (p. lo&>.The writer remarks that tlie Sylvian fissure ‘(makes a slight change in direction, continuing to extend backwards ” (p. 104),but thinks that the lateral sulcus of the Lemur is absent.

He represents awell-developed postlateral sulcus, such as is not found in my specimen. Concerning this he says :-“ A small sulcus runnin: in a transverse direction on tho upper surface of the hemisphere, at the junction of the parietal and occipital lobes, recalling the ‘temporo-occipital ’(scissure perpeudiczcluire exiei-ne)of the Quadrumana, whic11 lissure is, however, wanting in the smaller American Apes, as in Lemzcr ” (p. lob). We have seen above, however, that it sometimes occurs in Lemur, especially in thu species varius ; and even when absent as a separate element it is probably represented in tlle ‘‘tail ” of the lateral sulcus, both in Lemur and i!~cticebzcs.

In the specimen in the College of Surgeons Xuseum (fig. 11)the postlaterill sulcus is a separate element on the left hemisphere, but is merged in the lateral sulcus on the right side.

The upper end of the postsylvian sulcus bends forward, as it does in many Lemurs. On the right hemisphere it presents an additional ramus parallel to the lateral sulcus. In both hemisplieres there is a little sulcus behind the upper end of the postsylvian

Thus the upper end of the sulcus was bent forward on the left hemisphere ; but bifurcated, and its chief Lranch bent back on the right side. There is also an inferior ternporal sulcus.

I’sych. sxi-iii. p. 901) describes the same brain of Perodicticzcs as mvself; but as I liad to remove the arachnoid and pitt membranes before it was possible to study the arrangement of the sulci, liis account can hardly be regarded as an accurate description. Perodicticzm (Royal Collegc of Siirgeons, no. 295) reveals n pattern identical with that seen in the actual brain, which I haw just described.

In the little brain of Loris grcrcilis the olfactory parts closely resemble those of the other two genera of Lorisinae. The upper ends of tlie supra- and postsylvian suki approach very near to one another on the left hemisphere ; on the right hemisphere the former is prolonged into a hook surrounding the upper cod of the postsylvian. There is a simple arcuate lateral sulcus and a small postlateral. There is a small oblique orbital sulcus; and a shallow furrow placed far forward may represent the cororinl snlcus.

There is also a small inferior temporal sulc~zson the left hemisphere, as in the brain of Nycticebus. On the mesial surface tlie typical calcarine group and shallow intercalary and collateral sulci arc found.

Beddard examined the brain in two specimensof Loris grncilis, and stated that “there

are only three fissures plainly visible-the Sylvian, tlie angular [lateral3, and the antero-

temporal [postsylvian] ” (Proc. Zool. SOC.1895, p. 145). [His representation of thc brain (fig. 3) is, however, so studded with innumerable spider-like figures as to bc quite unintelligible.] In one of his specimens there was a separate postlateral [his “parieto-occipital ”I sulcus on one hemisphere.

Gervais’s illustration (Journ. de Zoologie, t. i. pl. 2. fig. 2) is useful as a demonstration of the actual size and shape of the brain in Loris; but Beddard’s criticism of his representation of tlie sulci is quite justified. *4mon,othe Lemurs it is possible, if the investigator has studied the effect of brain-form on tlie cranial wall in some members of the Order, to form a very good idea of the conGgurntion of the actual brain from an esamination of the brain-case. But Gervais has not exercised sufficient caution in the representation of the plaster casts, and gives an altogether erroneous idea of the configuration of the brain.

Ziehen’s account of the brain in this genus was based upon the specinieii in the Royal College of Surgeons and another specimen of his own. He represents the lateral aspect of the left heniisplrere (Arch. f. Psychiat. sxviii. fig. 6, p. 908). The Sylvian fissure, orbital sulcus, a very short postsylvian sulcus and a “ Scheitelstuck ” above it, a lateral and postlateral sulcus areall shown ; but in addition there is a transverse sulcus labelled €, not present in Beddard’s specimens nor in that in the College of Surgeons. The letter 4 is used by Ziehen to designate the central sulcus in Peroclicticus, and both the sulcus which I callf and that designated x in ,Vycticebzis.

transverse furrows (in Beddard’s specimen there is only oue, which is labelled “parietooccipital ”) representing the postlateral (transverse occipital) sulcus. The anterior end of the sulcus extends downward in front of the Sylvian fissure for a short distance, so as ;ilmost to reach the caudal end of a sagittal sulcus (coronalis). The latter is bent in an obtuse angle in the brain I hare examined; in Beddard’s specimen it is apparently represented by two separate sulci (labelled “infero-frontal ”) in the same straight line ; and in the skull of Kirk’s Galago there is a ridge representing an extensive straight siilcus directed obliquely forward and mesially, as in Lewzzw. Above the sulcus coronalis there is a faint depression (f),such as is found in Lemur.

On the mesial surface the sulci are grouped as in Garnett’s Galago. There is, however, a sulcus ci.

From an examination of the skull of GaZugo Budeiri (Bogal College of Sur,rreons,-

specimen So. 285, Osteological Series), I can definitely state that the brain is of the .%me shape as that of Garnett’s Galago, and presents rhinal and Sylvian fissures and a n orbital sulcus of the same type. There must have been, however, an extensive, though sltallow, postsylvian sulcus, the upper end of which curves upward and forward toward a lateral sulcus which was prolonged unusually far back. There was also an extensive coronal sulcus, like that of Lemw.

In a skull of Gulugo AlZeizi the most noteworthy feature is the impression of a crescentic or boomerang-shaped coronal sulcus.

The cerebral hemisphere of the small brain of Hicrocebas Sv12ithi resembles that of GaZugo in shape. It is 15.5 mm. long (wit,liout the olfactory bulb, which projects almost 3 mm. in front of the hemisphere) ; its maximum depth is 10 mm., and its greatest breadth 7.5 mu. (each hemisphere). The lateral caudo-venhl margin is

[Figure 31]

###### MORPHOLOGY OF THE BR,AIS IS THE MAJIMSLIA. 349

13.6 rum. long, and is so oblique that its lower end (measured in projection) is 10 rnm. further forward than its upper end. The natiform eminence of the pyriform lobe is a very narrow conical projection on the base of the hemisphere. As in the genns Lemur, and unlike the condition found in the Lorisin=, the orbital excavation extends back to the Sylvian fissure.

The thin, verticdlj-placed, leaf-like olfactory bulb resembles that of the Lorisin=. The rhinal fissure is almost wholly obliterated. The Sylvian fissure, which is constituted like that of Galago and the Lorisinxh, extends obliquely upward and backward on to the dorsal surface, and divides the lattcr into a broadly expanded posterior part (which extends as a thin leaf over the corpom quadrigemina and the anterior part of tlie cerebellum) and a narrow tapering anterior part.

There is a typically triradiate group of calcarine, retrocalcarine, and paracalcarinc, sulci, the greater portions of which are situated in the mesencephalic fossa on the lomcr surface of the caudal region of the hemisphere.

There are no other sulci.

The notes on the brain in Microcebns and CI&ogaZe in tlie oft-quoted memoirs of Gervais, Beddard, and Zielien add little more. Beddard records the presence of a very\small lateral (his “angular”) sulcus (Proc. Zool. SOC.1898, p. 147).

Practically all that is known of the structureof the brain in this subfamily is contained in that part of the ‘Histoire Xaturelle des Mammif&res’ of 5131.Rlph. Milnc-Edwards and Alfr. Grandidier which forms volume vi. of Alfr. Grandidier’s great Histoire de Madagascar.’ The otlier memoirs which refer to the brain of any of the 1ndrisin:e-

1iz., those of Zuckerkandl and Chudzinski-either add nothing new or are borrowed from Milne-Edwards’s great work. Flatau and Jacobsohn derive their account of the brain of Ilzdris from Chudzinski, to whom tlicy accord all the credit for a researell which is either wholly or for the greater part obviously a reproduction of the account given by 3Iiloe-Edn.ards.

Gervais describes moulds of tlie cranial cavity in the Indrisirm.

He dozs not SeCk11, however, to have exercised su6cient care in the study oE the impressions whiclo correspond to the cerebral sulci. For in the Lemurs, and especially in the genera Pi*opitheczcsitri(1 IwZris, it is possible to accurately map out all the sulci on the outer surface of the cerebral hemisphere, i f a critical examination be made d the prominent bony ridges on the iniier face of the cranium or of the furrows produced by these ridges on a mould of the cranial cavity.

I have examined a considerable series of crania OY the Indrisintc in the British Nuseum and in the Collection at tlie ltoyill College of Surgeons, and have thus been able to gain a much more accurate conception of the plan of the sulci than that conveyed by ttic lithographs which illustrate the ‘ Histoire de Malagascar.’

The mesial surface of tlie hemisphere in the Indrisinae io known to me only in the tlramings (Hist. Xadag. (2) tome vi. pl. 86. fig.6, pl. 87. figs. 2 b and 4 c j of MilneEdwards’s monograpli. It closely resembles thit of Chironys, which will be described later on in these notes.

Nothing is known of tlie smaller brain of AEahis except the scanty information contained in Rlilne-Edwards’s mcmoir. The plan of tlie ridges ou the iuterior of a cranium of Avahis l a n i p r (in the Hoysl College of Surgeons) is shown in fig. 31.

There are well-defined representatives oE tile Sylvian, postsylvian, and or1)ital sulci, shallow lateral and postlateral fnrron-s, and also a deep sulcus x (in apparent continuity with tlie lateral sulcus) running parallel to tlie Splvian fissure. Comparison with the brain of Iiiclris (fig. 30) seems to point to an homology with the element 1”’ in that genus. Comparison with the plan found in Propitlieem (compare fig. 24) seems to suggest its identity with the sulcusfof that genus. In Milne-l3dn.ards’s speciineii it isnot so distinctly transverse in direction. In niy specimen there is no trace of any ridge correspondiug to the coronal sulcus. All this varialdity of the furrows around tlie adjacent ends of the lateral and coronal sulci in the liidrisinze sliuws how unstable this region is in these animals. In tlie first place, this area is the seat of very pronounced growth-changes in all the Primates, and especially the lowlier members of the Ordcr, because the excitable area of tile cortex is expanding to a rnucli greater extent tliari in other mammals. At tlie same time, tliere is in tile Lemurs, and especially the Indrisiuze, anothey and a greater disturbing factor, which is the pronounced tendency to a disintegration of composite sulci: this must be the vxpressioii of some retrogressive process of evolution. This variability is perhaps best fbsemplified by the behaviour of the sulcus J; which seems to be linked in different individuds of the I n d r i h z to the coroiial sulcus, the sulcus e, and even to the lateral bI1lcus.

This instability is prob2l)ly due to two distinct factors.

###### THE; CEREBRAL HEMISPHERESIN THE GENUSDAUBENTONIA

(more commonly called Chiromp).

The ?)rain of the Aye-aye (Dct~ibeiitoniccnzadagascariensis, Geoff., Chironzys iaadagascwiensio, Cuv.) is of extraordinary interest to the morphologist by rcasoii of the possession of what Cliudzinski calls ‘‘contradictory characters.” ‘ h e same writer iurther explains this remark by the ststenient that although the Aye-aye is 110 larger than a small ra,bbit, or even not so big, it has a most voluminous brtiiu, very rich iu cerebral folds *. He adds that “the .form of the brain is ovoid, but its anterior cbxtrernity is markedly rounded. In this respect it resembles the brain of certsin ltodentia (Castor, Hystriz) and even certain Xarsupials (Phascolowys).” uthough these comparisons are quite justified, resemblances of a closer character are to be found \Iithin the Prosimiae themselves. Gervais describes tlie crdnial cast of the Aye-aye as being “plus arrondi et plus bomb6 B sa face supbrieure que celui des illakis, du Yropitldqueet de1’Indri’’+. The plan of tlie furrows is so puzzling that no writer on the brain of Chiromys has ventured to coinpare it in detail with that presented by other Lemurs. The chief reason for all this hesitancy in comparing the sulci of this brain with those of the other Yrimates depends upon the fact that in Chi?*ornysthe suprasylviCtn sulcus has become widely separated from the pseudosylvian sulcus, and the latter is exhibited i n different specimens in various stages of disintegration. Thus there is no typical Sylvian fissure; and the failure to recognize that the latter consists (in the other Yrimates) of an nppureitt blending of the suprasylvian and pseudosylvian sulci by the submergence of the region between the overlapping parts of these furrows (vide supra) naturally gives rise to confusion when the intervening area is rcot submerged, as is the case in Chiromys. Oudemans explains this esceedingly puzzling condition in these words :-“ Die Fissura Splvii zeigte sicli an cler rechtcn Hemisphiire vie1 mehr ausgepriigt als an der linken. SUPder obere Theil derselben ivar ticf einschneitlend, tlcr untere mehr sirperficiell, besonders an der linken Hemisplitire, wo 1)eidc ‘l’heile selhst getrennt aaren. Das wir es hier jedocli gewiss niit der Fissura Sylvii zii thun hal)cn, ergflb sich durch Vergleichung mit den Gehirnen einiger anderen Lemuriden.” (Katurh. Verh. Akad. Amst. xxviii. p. 25.)

The closing phrase is true, but tlic writer misses the obvious inference, which must follow from this important fact, viz.-that the Syll-ian fissure of the Lemurs is partly represented by the suprasylrian sulcus. I n order to evadc this conclusion, in deference to the popular belief in the identity of the so-called “ Sylvian fissure ” of Carnivora and the Prosimian Sylvinn fissure, Outlemans is forced to call the lateral sulcus “suprasylvian.” [For if the real suprasylvian is considered as representing the “feline Sylvian,” it is not illogical to call the lateral sulcus by the name ‘‘suprasplvian,” eren if such an hypothesis makes it necessary to suppose that tlie lateral sulcus has disappeared.] I t was unfortunake for Oudemans that he did not chance to see such a specimen as my example u ; for then there could have been no doubt that the Lemuroid Sylrian fissure is also in great part identical with the suprasylvian sulcus.

Chudzinski, who happened to have a specimen which (so far as I am able to judge from his unsatisfactory account) resembled my specimen u, failed to recognize the suprasylvian sulcus as tlie Lemuroid ‘‘Sylvian.”

He regards the pseudosylvian sulcus as the Sylyian fissure, and says that it, is joined to the rhinal fissure (which he calls the fissure of the olfactory lobes) on the right side but not on the left (Bull.SOC.Anthrop. s8r. 4, vii. pp. 13 ‘c 1-1.).[This difference bctween the two hemispheres also seems to occur in niy specimen u,but the condition of the brain prevents me from speaking more decisirelp.] He is then so puzzled lip the suprasylvian and postsylvian sulci, that he does not attempt to comptirc them with the sulci of the Primates, but, like all previous writers, scelis an analogy to the condition found in tlic Carnivores (op. cit. p. 16). His specimen seems to ha\-e been exceptionally interesting, and it is therefore very disappointing to find such imperfect drawings and such vague descriptions, from which it is impossible to form a clear conception of the condition. The postsylrian sulcus seems, if 1 read the account correctly, to be independent of the suprasylvian on the right side ; and the latter appears to be so related to the pseudosylvian as to be very slightly if at all differentfrom the normal Prosiniian Sylvian fissure [but tllis is by no means clear].

One cannot fail to be amazed that Chudzinski, who duriug the previous year had been studying the brain in Lernzir, n’y~ti~ebzts,and Indris, should have failed to recognize in the suprasylvian sulcus of Chiromys the homologuc of the upper part oE the Sylvian fisslireof these Lemurs.

It is difficult to form a clear conception of the condition of Owen’s specimen from his imperfect lithographs and vague description ; and the interpretations of’ it by succeeding writers (such as Pansch, Beddard, and Parker) only tend to still further confuse the issues.

[Figure 41]

TIic remarks of Germis arc of intcrcst only in so far as thcy call attention to the presence of an additional arcunte sulcus [;. e. tlie sepzration of tht? suprasylvian and the pseudosyvlvian sulci] without suggesting an>-solution.

In my specimen a the latf?ral sulcus liegins posteriorly at a point 8 mni. from the mesial plane and an equal distance from the posterior margin; the anterior extremity is 11.5 nim. from the mesial plane and 16 mni. from the anterior extremity of the hemisphere. In none of my specinieas, nor in that of Oudemans, cm I find any trace of a separate postlateral siilcus, but in the left ltemisphere of the specimen p there is a characteristic mesially-bent tailpiece such as we have seen representing the postlateral sulcus in many Lemurs.

The sulcus is 23 mm. long.

The furrows which are situated in that region of the hemisphere mhicli lies in front of tlic lateral and suprasplvian sulci arc su1)ject to considerable variation. On the left liemispliere of the spccinien /3 there is a typical orbital sulcus, and in a more dorsal position a wry obliquely placed sulcus, which a comparison with other Prosimian brains clearly si1ow.s to be the anterior part of the coronal sulcus. On the right liemisphcre of tlic same brain a shallow- depression unites these two furrows. On the left Iicmisphei*c of spccinien a they are joined to form a single transversely-placed mcuate Currow; on the right hemisphere the orbital furrow has disappeared and there is simply a transi-crse corond element. In two of the crania (302 and 3 0 2 ~to) which I have referred, the two sulci are well developed and uuitecl to form figures, one like a crescent, thc other like a lettcr U placed on its side.

These sulci are eslii1)ited in a typical manner in Oudemans’s memoir, and the names “ presylvian ” and “ prorean ” v-hich he applies to them u-c shall find in thc subsequent discussion to bc not inappropriate.

Midway between the sulcus which I have called coronal and the anterior ends of tlie lateral and suprasylvian sulci respectively, there are two sulci in the left hemisphere of the specimen 13. The upper of these may be compared with the sulcus distinguished by tlic letterfin L~nziri-,nnd the lower one with that called c. Tlicse two sulci are united by it shallow furrow to form a long transverse “sulcus centralis ” (fig. 35).

On tbc right heinisphere of the same brain (fig. 36) the two furrows e andfare much smaller and are widely separated. The sulcus f is joined to tlie anterior end of the 1:iteral sidcus, and e remains as a short independent furrow widely separated from a11 the other sulci on the hemisphere.

On tlic left hemisphere of thc specinien a (fig. 33) the two sulci e andfare united at an obtuse angle, and the former has a horizontal ramus, which rccalls the fact that it is really the posttrior fragment of tlie coronal sulcus. On the right hemisphere of this brain tile horimntul sulciis is iudependent of the moderately long central one.

On the riglit hemisphere of Oudemnns’s specimen there is a small triracliate sulcus_f (which he calls ‘‘coronal ”), and n horizontal sulcus exactly like that exhibited by the right hemisphere of my specimcn CL. This he calls “ prorean,” i. e. the same name hy which he denotes thc anterior element nhich I call “coronal.” This is of interest in so fi~asr lie gives the same name (‘I prorean ”) to these two widely-separated furrows, which I also regard as two fragments of one sulcus (“ coronal ’I). If me summarize all this puzzliii? 1-ariety in tlie arrangement of the furrows on tlir outer surface of tlie hemisphere of’ Chi~O????~ls,the great outstanding fact is revealed that, amidst all the variations nhicli so inodify as almost to disguise the identity of most of the sulci, tlie supi.sts>-lvianand lateral sulci alone remain nnchanged. They arc tlic deepest and most stable sulci on the outer surface of tlie hemisphcre.

The other feature of interest is the strongly markcrl tendency toward the formation of a transverse suleus from tlic elements c and f,which is either the hoiwologuc of tlie central sulcus or, at least, in the present state of our knowledge, fulfils all tlie knonn criteria of a sulcus of Rulaiido.

Tlie peculiar variations which have been noted iii the cerebral hemisphere of Chii*onzys

can only be explained on the supposition that a large and highly coiivoluted brain is imdergoing a process of rctrograde metamorphosis which leads to the disinteption of all except the most stable sulci. It is altogether incoiiccivable that the unstable group of fragnieiitory sulci which represent the pseudosglvian, postsylvian, and coroiial sulci can be stages in tbe evolution of a inore complex pattern of furrows. 911 tliese facts point to the conclusion that Chi~0n.r;~~is not tlie lowly Carnivore-like primitive Lemur which most other writers on cerebral anatomy s~ippose,but a highly-specialized Priniate which has undergone most pronounced retrogressive changes, as the result of which all the inore variable features of its brain stand revealed and, by contrast, show tlie underlying common inammalian features, wliicli are the heritage of all the Meta- and Eutlieritl.

None of the writers who have described the brain of Chii-onays lias placed on record any information concerning the mesial surface of the hemisphere. I have examined this aspect in four examples.

The corpus callosum is 23 inm. longL, and wry plump, with cz considerably thicl<encd spienium. Its anterior part is bent don-nw-ard so as to be boonierang-shapec11aped (fig. 37). This is unlike that of the other Lemurs, and may possibly be due to a retraction of the anterior regions of the hemisphere, when the latter lost the iiiore pointed form irliicli it must have had in the immediate ancestors of CIzi~onzys. For the peculiar blunt anterior pole of the hemisphere cannot be primitive, and wlieii this process took place it probably influenced the form of the corpus callosuiv on tlie mesial surface, just as it has niodifietl the coronal sulcus on the lateral aspect of the heiiiisplierc.

The hippocanipus is distinguished by tlie possession of an unusuall~~large liippocaiiipal tubercle (fig.38), and also possesses that peculiar furrow (u) which we have seen in thc genus Lentzcr. I kiiom of no brain other than those of the Primates in which tliese two peculiarities are both found. The particular foriii they assunic in Cl&aoinys is distinctly Prosiniian.

Tlie most interesting feature 011 the nicsial surface is tlie grouping of the sulci.

The problem of mapping-out tlie pattern of the cerebral sulci in this brain is one of singular and unexpected difficulty. In most Lemurs and in many of the CebidE the positions and extent of the various siilci can be determined with the greatest ease 2nd certainty. But in the crania of most Apes the ridges which correspond to the position of the cerebral furrows become blurred and indistinct in the greater part of the surface, excepting only tlic anterior region, which contains sharply-defined elentiom representiny the sulci rectus, arcuatus, orbitalis, and fronto-orbitalis.

I f a series oE crania of Cercopithccidte be examined, it will be found in many of t h a w tliat all the ridges bchind that representing the sulcus arcuatus are so indistinct that it would not be easy to map out the sulci, if we did not happen to know the plan. It is a peculiar fact that the ridge for the Sylvian fissure, which is always so distinct in the L~MIU‘S,becomes almost completely obliterated in tlie crania of many Apes. -4ncl the crests representing the intraparietal, the prtraliel, ilnd, in a less iiiarlied degree, tlie central sulci share a like Fate.

,4similar state of affairs is unfortunately present in GZobiZemu?*; so that it is quitt: impossible to niap out the position of most of the sulci. -1s in the Apes, the ridges representing tlic sulci on tlie anterior reTion of the hemisphere are by far thc 12lost distinct.

TilLlsthere can be no doubt concerning the recognition of the orbital (a)and corona3 (0) sulci (figs. 39 S; 40). ‘here is a, prominent bony ridge representing a furrow skirting thc fronto-orbital margin of the hemisphere, wliich is probably the diagonal sulcus (e). Bllt; the deterniinat,ion of tlie identity of the other furron-soffers insuperable difficulties.

The form of the brain (especially the full rounded blunt antcrior ends of the hcinispheres and the absence of a fronto-orbital edge) resembles that of Cl~i~~onays.‘~%c arrangement of the furrows approximates most nearly to that seen in the Indrisinz and especially Ilidiis. There can be little doubt as to the identity of the suprasylvian, lateral, coronal, ancl postsylvian sulci, ancl tlie furrow f represents the siniilarly-labclled sulcus in otlier Lemurs. BurckII;LIY~~’Sextraordinary suggestion that the furrow which I have called “ lateral ” represents the precentr 11suluus i3 peculiar, because no central SLI~CLIS is present ; but it is preposterous to represent as the precentral a furrow which, according to his diagram (OF.cit. p. 233, fig. 2 a), is placed parallel to ancl on a lower plane than the frontal snlcus.

The apparent absence of the orbital sulcns is noteworthy.

But the most unwarrantable statements in Burclihardt’s work are those acetising I~orsytliXajor of mistaking the optic nerves for the olfactory bulbs. The 1;ttter imatornist had camfully studied and described the skull of -nlgalnclctpis * long b(:foi*e he attempted to describe the plaster mould of its cranial cavity. So that when he came to (lcscribe tlie cranial cast, there was no difficulty in recognizing as such the optic nerv-es (even though their position is so peculiar) and tlic olfactory pediuncles (greatly elongated though they are). It might perhaps he iniagined that the larger dimensions and more distinct separatioii of tlie lateral geiiiciilate body from the rest of the tlialainus arc due to the fact that the optic tracts arc unusually large in all the Lemurs. But this cannot be tlic whole explanation, because in many other large-eyed mammals (see ninny Rodents, Ungulates, mid others) there is no such distinctness of the external gcniculnte body as me find in all Lcmurs and other Primates. This plienomenon must be due to aii increased perfection of the cortical path for visual impulses, rather than to a mere enlargement of the lower optic tracts.

The four quadrigeimind bodies rise to tlie same level, and reseiiihle in shape those of ilic lUyrniecop11agidac inore nearly than tllosc of any other mnminals (excepting the othcr Yrimutcs). There is a wcll-defined tractus peduncularis traiisverms, but it calls for no further mcntion.

Ifthe region of tlie medulla oblongata in tlie Lemurs be compared witli that of other mammals of a corresponding size, tlie only notemort,liy distinctive feature of tlie former will be the relatively greater prominence of the olivary body. I t is, however, somrwhatl smaller and less prominent than it is in most Apes, the chief olivary nucleus hein? smaller in proportion to the internal accessory olivary nuclcus in the Lemurs than it is in the Apes.

It is commonly argued that there can be no calcarine sulcus in tlie Carnivora because there is no posterior cornu ; but the same writers clo not deny the calcarine nature of the analogously-placcd furrow in the Lemurs, even though there is no posterior coruu there. It is tliereiore very surprising to find Flower spealiing of “ the absence of anything reaenibling the calcarine SU~CUS” in the Cat’s brain in the same memoir in wliicli he made tlie important observation (quoted above) that the existence of a patent posterior cornu is not a necessary condition of‘tlie presence of a calcar, and therefore of a calcarine salcus. In the case of certain Carnivores, such as the Seals, a definite posterior cornu of the ventricle is found, and in some cases, e. g. I’hcn, it reaches large dinicnsions : in these animals the ‘‘ splenial bulging ” in the lateral ventricle so closely ‘(simulates ” the “calcar -’of the Primates that there can be no reasonable doubt as to their identity, This fact W;LS well known early in the last century, for Serres’s observations, that “ the hippocampus ininor[cnlcar] has hitherto been obserred only in Man,” which continue &‘.. .. During my investigations on the brain in the Eclentata, thc results of which weuA presented to this Society almost four years ago, I was very much astonished to find a well-defined swelling on the mesial all of tlie lateral ventricle of 01-ycteropzrs which simulated the calcnr avis. My astonishment n’asdue to the fact that at that time I was not acquainted with any of tlie above-mentioned writings and knew only of the teaching of such anatomists as Flower, Turner, ancl Cunningham, 117110 have so formally and categorically deiiied the possibility of homologizing the calcarine sulcus of the Primates 11ith the splenial furrow in other mammals. Nevertheless the mass of evicleiice in f’avour of the identity of tliese two furrnws, which a study of the Edentata and other mamimsls 3;ielded, seemed to me to be so orerwhelmiiig, that I adopted this iriew emn though it is in direct contradiction to the ~ i e w sof those anatomists, from whom I have acquired much of my liuowledge of the nianimalian brain f.

-1niong the heterogeneous collection of EdentaEa, there are found all stages in the form of the calcarine sulcus intermediate between the Carnil-ore condition on the one hand, and the simpler form found in the Chiroptera, Jlarsupialia, and Insectivora (i.e. if Guleopitkecus can be called an Insectivore). In no true Insectivores is there a calcarine, or “splenial ” (as most anatomists w odd call it) sulcus. But this is not iiecessarily

of any great significance, because in the snialler Chiroptera and (Yolyprotodont) JIarsupials there is no such sulcus, altliougli the larqer inembers of these Orders (Pterops, Cyzoizycteris, l’liylaciizzcs, and practicallj7 all tlie Diprotodontia) present :Ltypical one.

The most peculiar fact, so far as the distribution of this furrow is coiicerned, is its absencc in most of tlie Roclentia. I n only one or two of the largest members of the orders, such as IIydrocJmv*zis,is any such furrow developed ; and even in these few cases it is sinall and insignificant. I n the JLouotreniata tlic calcarine sulcus cannot be recognized ; moreover, it is highly probablo that none of the mesial sulci in the liemisphcre of Echicl~mrepresents tlic calcarine sulcus of tlie RIetatheria and Eutheria.

“hippocanipus minor,” as it supposed distinctive feature of the human brain, led to a very heated controversy in 1S61, during thc c o ~ m eof which many anatomists subjected tlie “ occipital ” region of the brain in the Primates to a careful examination. One of the most valnnlde results of this unseemly strife was the introduction of the useful term ‘‘calcarinc sulcus,” n-hich Huxley conferred upon the external furrow, the obverse of which forins the calcar avis in thc lateral ventricle. Another result was the dtwonstration b y Hnxley, Plower, and others, that the calcar aud its sulcus are not distinctive of the limiau lxain, b u t are cciiimoci to all the Apes or even Primates, according to Flower*.

The c:rlcar was first described early iu the eighteenth century by 3Iorand f, ml:o

likened thc little projec5ioii on tlie mesial nall of the posterior cornu of the lateral ventricle (in the human brain) to a cock’s spur and called it “krgot,” tllc Latin equivalent of wliic‘li is “calcccr ncis.” Towards the latter part of tlie same century Vicq-d’_\zyr$ called it “ 7rQqjocctqJzis WZ~KAOY,” because it appeared to be continuous with the larger swelling known as hi~ipoccimpiu[n20j01*].

I have introduced these historical notes here, because it has unfortunately become nccessary to eniphasizc once niorc, firstly, the contention of Huxley that the term ‘‘hippocampus minor ” should be for ever banished v ith the niisconception which it syinbolizes ; ancl that the term “ calcar avis,” or simply “ calcar,” be substituted ; and, secondly, that the true and exact significance of the term “sulczcs cnlecwinzcs” may not be forgotten, as it is by most modern writers on the anatomy of the brain. As the name “ calcarine ” is derived from ‘‘calcar,” it necessarily follows, as Huxley showed, that the essential pui-t of the szdczis nad that strictly desewing the title “ cctlcariiae”is thefwrow zohichp‘oduces the culcc~rnuis. But Husley hiinself pointed out that the SU~CUS‘‘extends beyond the calcar and the posterior cornu”7 . And he also did much more than this. Within recent years Cunningham* has called attention to a differentiation of tlie eaicariiie sulcus into anterior and posterior elements, which are to be distinguished the one froin the other not only by a cliffereiice in the time and mode of development, but also, in many cases, by their features in the adult human brain. The “ anterior calcarine ” sulcus is the first to develop and becomes an extremely deep sulcus, which is ~vholly rcy~oiisiblef or the bulging of the calcar in the ventricle. In other worcls, it is the true ‘I calcariiie sulcus.”

The sulcus which Cunningham calls “ posterior calcarine ” develops later and quite independently of the anterior sulcns ; it never becomes as deep as the former ; as a rule it, does not share in the formation of tlie calcar, and in inany cases it is separated from ilie anterior or calcarine sulcus by a submerged fold of cortex.
