adds in explanation that he "calls it sillon CLLICLW~~Z,although the eiyot (calcar) from which its nanie is derived exists only in the Primates " (p. 322). It is clear from this that Broca believes that the postsplenial sulcusrepresents thal sulcusdf the Primate brain which produces the cslcar, i.e. the true calcarine and not the retrocalcarinc sulcus.

I n the brains of most Carnivora and Ungulata, as well as in many other manimals, a series of inconstant and exceedingly varialjle sulci malie their appearance behind the calcarine (i.e. the retrosplenial part of the spienial) snlcus. In the smaller members this so-called <' postsplenial " sulcus tends to run parallel to the "splenial " sulcus. But in tlie larger Carnivores, especially the Bears and Seals, and the larger Ungulatcs, such as the Cow, Horse, and Camel, there arc usually a series of postsplenial snlci ; and it iisually happens that the deepcst of these pursucs a course wliich is not parallel to the calcarine (splenial), but inclines obliquely npw':\rd and backn.arc1 at an angle with tlie latter. It not unfrequcntly happens that this "rctrcicalcarine " sulcus, as me may not inaptly tprm it, joins tlie calcarine. The tendency of the retrocalcariue cleuient to pursue a course which is not parallel to the calcarine becomes more pronounced in such mammals (the Seals, for example) as possess a distinct caudal (01' occipital) prolongation of the hemispherc. It is, therefore, not srrrprisiiig to fiud that in those mammals-1-iz.,

the Primates-in which such a caudal extension of the lieinispliere is most pronounced (so that the meclianical factors which tend to induce the cortex tq become folded in the longitudinal dircction arc most potent), tlie retrocalcarine sulcus d\va!-s assumes the longitudinal, and never the vertical, direction *. It is still fiirther determined in this course by the increasing obliquity of the cnlcarine sulcus, which is brought about partly by the backmard extension of the l~cniisphcreitself, and partly by tlie caudal elongation of the corpus callosum. In fact, the direction of the calcarjne arid retrocalcarine sulci is brought, as the result of tlie opcration of these various factors, so accurately into tlie saiiie line that in most Primates the less important retrocalcarine element seems to develop merely as a caudal prolongation of the more precocious and stable calcarine suicus.

The brain of the Camel affords a striking illustration of thcse facts.

"lie mesial \nrface of its cerebral hemisplicre exhibits a triradiate group of deep suki behind the spleiiium of the corpus callosuiii (fig. 55). Superficially these furrows appear to be ,joined to one another ; but if their lips be scprated, it will bc found in some cases that tllc deep cdcarine sulcus is confluent witli iieitlivr tlie intercalary sulcus, as in most iiiaiiini;11s,nor with the retrocalcarine sulcus, as in most Primates, hut is separated froiii both by deeply submerged gyri, as is frequently the case in the human brain. It is, moreover, noteworthy that the bulging (calcar) which is pushed into tlic definite posterior cornu of the ventricle in this brain is produced wliolly by the calcarine, and not by the retrocalcarine nor intercalary sulci.

* The variability of this sulcus clearly sliowv t,liat it posacssos little stability, and cannot be regarded as i~ "lchnitely fixed landmark like tile truc c:ilcarinc. It probably arises in ortlcr to relieve the tension of' the ~spmiding"visual '' cortex, aiid it,s form and position arc detcrtuiried by colditions which arc chiefly, if not wl~oll~-, uit.chanica1. 111thcse respects it prcseiits a most marked coiitrast to the calcarinc sulcus.

In his \vell-knoTvn memoir "On the Convolutions of the Brain," Sir William Turner * gam expression to very decided opinious conccrning the calcarine sulcus and posterior cornu. Writing about tlie brain of IIupZe jucchus, lie states :-'' There is no splenial fissure, but opposite the splenium a distinct cnlcarine fissure " (p. 141). Tliis amounts to a categorical denial of the homology of the calcarine and spieniztl sulci.

On the same page lie makes the furtlier statement (in this case referring to the brain of &>cticebus [Xteimps])that "the calcarine fissure ...proves the presence of both posterior cornu and calcar avis "T. The data upon which this statement is based are borrowed from Flower (Phil.Trans. 18G2),who says, in the memoir quoted, that he found it impossibie to determine in this particular brain "n-hethcr or not the posterior cornii exists." Kerertheless Turner goes 011 to say that, "if the surface of the hemisphere be examined with the view of deterininin5 tlie presence of an occipital lobe by the evidence of a fissure, the calcarine fissure is that which is to be regarded as of primary importance " (p. 141). Tliis means presumably that there is no "occipital lobe " in any mammals other than tlie Priiiiates, seeing that the "calcarine fissure " is supposed to be absent. The spleuial sulcus, which occupies the position of the calcarine sulcus, is regarded by Turner as merely a part of tlie calloso-marginal sulcus (p. 144).

In his discussion of the homologies of the calcarine group of sulci, Ziehen coinpares the furrows of the Yrosimian brain with those of the Carnivora, and especially Phoccr hispirla. Like Broca, he regards the posterior branch of tlie splenial sulcus (-‘rainus horizontalis posterior ”) as the calcarine sulcus, and tlie ‘I occipito-temporal part of (he splenial ” as the “ stem of the f. parieto-occipitalis and fissnra calcarina ” (p. 919). The confusion in these statements arises from the failure to recognize that it is the so-called ‘‘stem of the parieto-occipital and calcarine furrows ” which alone deserves the title “ calcarine ”f, whereas the furrow he calls “ calcarine ” is really the retrocalcarine. But if the calcarine sulcus of the Prosimiz is represented by the end piece of the splenial sulcus in the Carnivora, why does he consider the obvious liornologue of the latter in Pteropzls to be the sulcus a (calloso-marginal or intercalary), aiid not the sulcus p (calcarine) ?

9similar confusion of the true calcarine sulcus and the posterior calcarine has been made by thc German Anatomical Nomenclature Commission, calling the former “ f. occipito-calcarina ” and the latter “f. cdcarina,” in spite of the fact that Huxley introduced the name ‘‘calcariiie ” to distinguish that sulcus which produces the calcar,

- i. e. the so-called ‘‘occipito-calcarine.’’ Burt Wilder, who has disagreed with thc conclusions of the German Committee on so many otker points, seems to elzlhody the same confusing use of terms in his system of nomenclature.

Many other ’writers, such as Benedikt and Neynert, apply the term calcarine to that portion of the sulcus which can only by courtesy, as it were, be so called, because it is really a separate sulcus, the retrocalcarine, which has secondarily become confluent with the true calcarine. If it were not for this confusion of terms, the conclusions at which these writers have arrived might have been received as an approximately accurate expression of the hoivologies existing between the furrows of the Carnivora and the Primates ; but this can hardly be said of them in their present confusing form $, Yansch’s criticisin of the teaching of Meynert is utterly futile in the light of our present knowledge. The climax of his argument -“Wo ist bei einen Raubthier ein Calcar avis ? ”-becomes meaningless, if we compare figs. 51 and 53 of this work 4.

The works of the English anatomists Turner and Cunningham are in direct conflict with all the other writings quoted above. I have already referred to Turner’s views.

C~~nninghamliassaidthat “we are not in aposition at present to offer an opinion . . .

upon the statement thnt the calcariue fissure is also developed in brains below tlie Primates ” *. Five years lnter lie slated that, “except in the case of certain of the main furrows ( P . 9. Sylvinn, hippocaiiipal), it is very questionable indeed if there is any homological correspondence 1)etween the sulci of a primate brain and the sdci of a quadrupedal l~-ain.” I n spite of all the research in this field siiice these quoted passages n-ere written, Cunninghnni has recently reaffirmed his unaltered belief in his view in 1hese words :-“ TVe now linow- that tlie priinate cerehruni was not only distinguished from that of all lower niamiiials by the possession of a distinct occipital lobc, hut also l)y having imprinted on its snrfacc a convolutionary desiqii tvhich, in all but a few l‘unclamental details, mas different from that of any other order of niamiiials ” f-.

Cunningharn Iiiniself does not tell us what he means by the expression. If it is nierely the caudal 1)rolongation of tlic heniisphere above the cerebellum, then ma2uy large Carnivores have ill1 equal or perhaps better right to be regarded as Primates than some of tlie Lemurs ; and if it is the posterior coimu which is the diagnostic sign, then the Leiniirs cannot be regarded as Primates, whereas the Seals, the Camel, and sonie Cetacea are to be classed along with tlie Apes. If, however, it is the calcariiie SU~CUSwhich, to quote Turner, “ determines the presencc: of an occipital lobe,” how caii we draw a line of demarcation i n respect to this feature between the Primates and other mammals? So far as I an1 aware, these are the only possible critelia of an “occipital lobe,” and none of‘ them, eirher separately or in conjunction, can be regarcled as exclusively distinctii-c of the Primates.

What is this occipital lobe, which is so distinctive of the Primates ?

If me refuse to adinit the hoinology of the cdeariue sulcus of the Primates and the retrospleiiial part of tlie spleiiial sulcus of other niamrnals, we have a far inore difficult probleni to solve. For an explanation is then needed of the reasons for rcgsrding as different two sulci (in different groups of mammals) presenting identical relations to the lateral ventricle and to the neighlionring brain-regions, a similarly pwcocious appearance in ontogeny, aiid a constancy under varying conditions in nianiinals u hich have widely diverged from the primitive stock. Why should we suppose that the \plenial sulcus, which is the most constant and most precocious neopillial furi*ow in the inesial mall of the hemisphere in tlic Jlarsupialia, Cliiroptera, Edentata, Carnivora, Ungulata, and Cetacea, fails t o develop in the Primates, and that another aiid a different furrow develops in the Primates iiz a positiou which exactly correspoizcls to that occupied 117the splenial in other niamrnals and at the scme epoch in developineiit ? Such a l)lienoiinenon is utterly inconceivable. The calcarine and the retrospleiiial part of the splenial sulci are certainly identical.

But, it inay bc asgued, the calcarine sulcus is an independent furrow, whereas tlie ‘‘ spleiiial ” is prolongtxl into a supracallosal course--the intercalary SU~CUS. But in the ILyrniecophagidae, Bradypoclidz, and Manidze the calcarine is not joined to tlic inlcr-

calary sulcus, and iii some of the smaller Marsupials, such as T~ichosurus,there is no intercalary sulcus, tlie simple calcarine furrow alone being present.

It may further be objected that a true calcarine sulcus can exist only when there is a posterior cornu of tlic lateral wiitricle. If so, the Lemurs cannot have a calcarinc sulcus, whereas the Seals and the Camcl possess such a farrow.

It is quite impossible to frame any definition of a calcariiie sulcus or of an ‘‘occipital lohe ” which can be raised as a harrier between the Primates aiid the other maiiii-ual~,

such as Cunningham would liavr: us erecl. The calcarine sulcus and tlie calcar avis are not tlie exclusive property of tlie Primatcts, but are the coniinon heritage of all the Jlctatheria and Eutheria.

In most mammals this sulcus becomes joined to the intercalary, a furrow of little 1iioq)hologicalimportance ; but in the Primates the backward prolongation of the corpus callosum so alters the direction of the calcarine that it forms an acute angle with thc livie of the intercalary, so that for purely rncchanical reasons the two furrows fail to unite, and the calcarine becomes confluent with the retrocalcarine, wliich is another €urrow of quite seconclary importance.

It is not without consitlerable significance that these phenomena occur in an equally pronounced form in the Lemurs as in tlie Apes, in spite of the fact that tlie nieclianicrtl conditions favouring such a sundering of the calcarine-intercalary junction are at least equally marked in m a ~ yCarnivores, in which the separation does not occur. It would, however, be unwise to attach too great an importance to this fact. because we find the calcariiie sulcus separated from the intercalary in the Anteaters, Sloths, and Pansolins.

The fact that no trace of the paricto-occipital sulcus is i‘ound iu the Hapalidze and scT-erai of the smaller Cebidz seemed to suggest tlie possibility of that furrow being a purely Simian feature, which had become evolved in the Apes only. I was therefore inclined at first to follow Flower’s teaching, and regard the paracalcarine sulcus of the Lemurs as one of the limbs of tlic bifid extremity of tlie retrocalcarine sulcus of the Apes. But the fact that it branclies off from the point of union of the calcariiie and retrocalcarine sulci shows that it occupies a position analogous to that of the lower extremity of the paricto-occipital sulcus in the higher Apes.

But are we justified in calling this furrow parieto-occipital? Most writers do so n ithout the slightest hesit‘J,t’ion.

In order to settle this point I haT-e examined the brain in every genus of Xonkej-s, and h a ~ efcruud that in the liighcr Apes, where the true parieto-occipital sulcus of I-Iuman ilnatoiny can alone he said to exist, this furrow is coniposed of two and frequently more elements. I n the majority of eases, llovw-er, there are two s&i,

dorsal and veiztral, which overlap to a considerable extent, the intervening area of cortex usually becoming subinergccl so as to obscure tlie dual nature of llie resultant furro117. Tlic ventral furrow I have called “paracalcariiic,” because it pursues a course alongside tlie calcarine sulcus in its most typical form, e. 9. in Xinain. The dorsal sulcus is obviously due to the bias ~ l i i c his given to the developing cortex near tlie dorsal edge by tlie intrapaiietal sulcus. This furro~vbecomes so sharply bent that it foriiis an acute

5 i *

[Figure 76]

angle, which points toward the mesial surface, and so affects the neighbouring area that a vertical sulcus usually develops in response to this mechanical bias.

The paracalcarine SU~CUSin tlie Lemurs obviously represents the ventral element only, and not the whole of the parieto-occipital.

I have introduced the term “intercalary ” to distinguish a furrow which has hitherto received no exclusive title. In most mammals it is joined to the calcarine, and the complex is called “splenial” : in tlie Primates it is separated from the calcarine aiitl joined to the genus1 sulcus to form tlie calioso-marginal SU~CUS. In many Carnivores iL is also joined to the crucial sulcus. It is therefore intercalated between the three furrows-calcarine, genual, and crucial-to any or all of which it may be joined. 111 spite of its constancy in tlie i\iIamnialia, the intercalary furrow is morphologically nnstable and readily becomes broken up into several fragments in the larger Ungulates and Carnivores. A careful examination of the behaviour of this furrow in the wliolc Xammalia leads me to regard the paracalcariiie as being equivalent to the separated posterior fragment of the intercalary sulcus, or as a new compensatory-calcarine furrow developed only in the Primates. I n the case of sulci which possess so little individuality and morphological value as the intercalary and the parieto-occipital, it is of little use attempting to establish any exact correspondence, because this can hardly be said to exist even in two liemispheres of the same brdin. All lhat can be said with certainty is that the tension of the growing cortex, which would be relieved in tlie Carnivora by the confluence of the intercalary and calcarine sulci, is instrumental in forming the paracalcvine sulcus in the Lemurs. This does not, however, imply the ideiitity of the two furrows. The instability of the paracalcarine element is shown in the CebidE :

in Cc6lli%’wix it is either absent or very diminutive, in Ch~ysothTixit forms the typically vertical ventral element of the parieto-occipital sulcus, and in Nyctipitheczcs it is horizontal. Of course the latter cannot be described as homologous with thc typical furrow of Chrysothrix, but merely as the expression of the same causal factors. There can therefore be no strict homology between such an unstable furrow and any sulcus, and especially such a variable element as the intercalary, in other maiiimals.

When we consider haw fickle this sulcus is, the fact of its extreme constancy of form and relations in all the Lemurs, not excepting even the aberrant, T’amizcs and Chi?*on~ys, becomes a valuable testimony to the closeness of the bond of affinity which unites all the Yrosimize. In fact, a study of the mesial aspect of the hemisphere affords convincing proof of this undoubted kinship.

In many Lemurs of various genera an oblique sulcus (b)appears on the ventral side of thc retrocalcarine SU~CUS. A comparison of this with a series of Primate brains shows it to be the posterior part of that composite and ivorphologically unimportant furrow vhich is called cc collateral ” in the brain of Man and the Apes.

Innumerable attempts have been made, with results as varied as they are nuiiierouc, to solve the problem of determining to how great an extent, if at all, it is possilik to institute exact comparisons between the sulci of two brains.

Ifthe two hemispheres of any highly convoluted brain bc compared, a certain numboi*

of furrows -41 be €ound in each, which cannot be exactly homologized with any SLI~CLLS

in the other hernisphcre. And not unnaturally the number of such “ incomparable ” elements increases when we compare the hemispheres of different individuals, specir.s, genera, fandies, and orders. The crucial question is thus reduced to the enquiry as to the possibility of recognizing any snlci as the eoinnion properly of several Orders.

The most divergent and mutually contradictory views are held at the present time on this siibject. Thns there is the negative teaching, which is perhaps most forcibly and dogmatically expressed in a posthumous memoir by A. J. Parker in these words :“The mistake is often made ... .of attempting to compare their [the Mammalia in general] convolutional characters with those of the Primates, including Man .. . .TVf: cannot expect, nor do we fincl, any exact homolosical rclations between the convolutions of this phyllum [sic] and those of‘ other mammalian phylla [sic] ” *. The sainc vicw is expressed in a less proiiounced form hy D. J. Cunningham.

The 1-iew of Gegenbaur may be summed up in his own words by the statement “dnss eiiie Homologie nur in sehr cngen Greiizen besteht uncl bei sehr vielen gar niclit durclifuhrbai-ist ” (Vergl. Ailat. der Wirbelthiere, i. Band, 189S, p. 769).

,Uthougli in many of his earlier memoirs Turner had instituted comparisons betwem the sulci in different mammalian Orders, yet in his latest contribution he comes to the conclusion that in each Order of Afainmals a special pattern of sulci is evolve2 peculinr to itself.

Most otlier writers who have in any way dealt with this snbject have come to thc conclusion that it is possible to liomologize the snlci of Primates to a greater or lew extent with those of otlicr mammalian Orders, hut there is an infinite variety of sugqcstions as to the exact manner in n-hich this is to be done.

Before we consider a few of the suggested interpretations, it will conduce to clearness if I first explain, hy a reference t o tlie Cat’s brain, the iionienclaturc employed in describing the sulci in Orders other than tlie Primates.

The furrow commonly called ‘‘Sylvian fissure ” is an offshoot of the rhinal fissure : as the usual designation is undoubtedly erroneous, I shall call it the pseudosylvian sulcns (fig. 56 b ). The sulcus which is best known by the name “presglvian” (Ouxn), I have termed “orbital ” for reasons which will be apparent later. The furrow which is commonly called “posterior suprasylvian ” has been designated by its original name ‘‘postsylvian ” (Owen), not only because the latter is more appropriate, but chiefly that

It will be noticed that all of these writers (as well as those who arrive at negative results) attempt to institute direct comparisons between highly specialized representatives of tlie Carnivora like the Dog and Cat and tlie higher Apes. No writer has talien tlte obvious precaution of testing the accuracy of his suggestions by an appeal to the simplest and most generalized types in the two Orders : nor, again, is it altoyether clear why tlie vast majority of writers invariably seek for homologues for the sulci of the Primates in only one Order of BZtininials (in most cases the Carnivora), unless it be because the non-Primate type of sulci is seen in its most specialized form in these latter.

Every writer (including even those who, like Parker and Cunningham, deny the homologies of tlie sulci in thc Yriniates and other Orders) commits the fundamental error of regarding the so-called Sylviaii fissure of the Carnivora and other 1CIamnials as the representative of the Sylviaii fissure of the Primates. But there cannot be tlie slightcst doubt that the two furrvws are not homologous.

The only means of arriving a t any reliable conclusions as to the possibility of homologizing the sulci of the Leniurs with otlier maiiiinals is to critically study the beliaviour of these furroi~-sin the ~Iai-nnialiaas a whole. ###### THEJIOJWHOLOGYOF THE STLVIASFISSURE.

The Sylvian fissurc is found in its complete and typical form only in the human brain. The peripheral lips of three sulci 1)cconie opercular and approach until they meet : t l i c b peculiar pattern thus formed upon the surface by the nicetin: of these labia is commonly called the Sylvian fissurc.

The region which becomes oT-erlapr)ed by the opercula is called tlic insula Reilii and is bounded by three sulci-the inferior, superior, and anterior limiting sulci. If the human brain bc comparcd with a sufficiently complete series of brains of Primates, it uill become so obvious as to amount to an absolute demonstiation that, the inferior (inorphologically posterior) limiting sulcus represents tlie pseudosylvian, the superior limiting sulcus the suprasylvian, and the anterior limiting sulcus the diagonal sulcus, respcctively, of the Lemurs and other mammals.

It has been clearly shovn by Marchand * and Cunningham? that the anterior limiting sulcus of the island of Ecil is the hornologue of tlie fronto-orbital sulcus of the ,lnt~liropoidApes. If anyone is disposed to dispute this contention and follow the lead of Herv6, Eberstaller, TValdeyer, and Giacomini (anions othcrs), a glance at the series of hrains of Anthropoid Apes, and especially tliose of the Gorilla, in the JIuseum of tlw ltoyal College of Surgeons will convince him of' his error $. The anterior limiting sulcus of the insula is certainly the liomologue of tlie fronto-orbital sulcus of tlie Apes. Tlie latter is best developed in tlie genera AIit~~i.o~opithecus,Ximia, and lI~Zo6des,but is constantly found in a less well-developed condition in SemizopitJuxus, P'ciyio, arid frequently in othcr genera of Old-World ,l.pc's and sometimes also in the larger members of the family Ccbidx..

In the Hapalidte and the smaller Cebidz, and sometimes also in the smaller Ccrcol)ithecidae, this sulcus is absent. It I s therefore interestins to note its presence in mm.;indiriduals of the c~ccusL e m w as well as in some of tllc Indrisinm. Undue importance lnust not, lion-ever, bc attached to this fact, becuuse the fronto-orhital sulcus of tlic J,emurs and Apes is almost certainly thc liomologuc of the di.ig01ia1 siilcus of tlie Carnivora, Ungulata, and Zdentata.

1b.‘39), it represents part of tlie Sylvian fissiwc; according to Pansch it is the precentral sulcus; and according to Broca and E1)erstaller (and also Soury in liis great TI-ork ‘],e SFstkme NcrJ-eusCentral,’ 1899)it is the homologue of tlie central ( Xolando’s) sulcus.

‘The evidence of Comparative Anatomy ( a d by this I do not mean the fatile attcnlpt to compare the brain of one specialized CarniJ-ore like the Cat with that of the 1ligllest I’riiiiate) sliows decisively that tlie presylJ-ian sulcus o€ the hIamiiialia cannot possibly represented in the Primates by any of tliesc four sulci- Sylvian (Owen), 1)recentral (I’anscli), central (Broca), or fronto-orbital (Ziehcn). The orbital (presylvian) sulcus is 1)erhaps tlie most constant sulcus (in tlie neopnlliuiii) in tlie Jlammalia. t~op?mzis),and in all Rodents, Insectivores, a i d Marsupinls wllicli have any cerebral sulci. It is therefore liiglil?- improbable tllat such a stable sulcusslioultl either be entirely absent or be represented by an inconstant clement in the Primates. JCoreoi-er, t!le brain of tlitk

I’rimates Fosscsses a sulcus in the same position in the liemisphere as the prcsy1yia.n occupies in otlier inaminals ; and this sulcus is the iiiost coiistaiit furrow in front of tllc Sjlrianfissure. This can be no other than ilic orbital sulcus (the ‘‘triradiate ” sulcus of Turner ; the “presj-lrian ” sulcns of OUcn). Tlic correctness of this identity of presjll-iall and orbital sulci becoiiics more conclusively demonstrated as the range of coiiiparisol1 increases. If we compare, for esanil~le,the presyl\-ian sulcus of the ltoclcnt Bo~ichotis,

or of the Carnivoye Yiue~1~6or the Ungiilate P1’ocuzz’aYwith the orbital sulcus of such n Prosiiiiian brain as that of Gulugo or even Lenzur, one cannot deny that the two sulciare morphologically identical. I f \\ e examine a large series of human brains, it will be found that the stable portion of the orliitsl sulcus consists of a deep oblique sulcus, the posterior end of which approaches close to and. often joins the outer limb of bifurcation of the olfactory sulcus, just as the presylvian sulcus of the Carnivore’s joins the rhinal fissure ; but the simple linear form of the orbital sulcus of 3Ean is usually disguised by a series of inconstant branches, so that it seems to lose the simplicity of form it possesses in most Apes. If this fundamental part of the hnmaii orbital sulcus bc coiiipared with the prcsylrian sulcus of the larger members of the other Manimalim Orders, such, for example, as the Camel and the Seals, a demonstration of the identity of these two furrows will be afforded which is quite as striking as tlie similarity of the presylvian snlcus of I)oZic?zotis to the orbital of Galugo.

Such instances might be multiplied without limit.

So that, if we take into consideration all the facts which a study of the brain in nll mammals yields, there is a mass of evidence amouiitiny to absolute demonstration that the sulcus variously called presylvian, supraorbital, orbital, and triradiate in different mammals is funclamelitally one and the same furrow.

Between the orbital sulcus and thc lower end of the Sylvian fissure we occasionally find a furrow- in the Lemurs and the New-World Apes, and generally in tlie Old-World Apes : this is the fronto-orbital sulcus. With regard to this homology in the Lemurs, I quite agree with Ziehen. But, on the othcr hand, the evidence which I have been ablc So collect points conclusively to the truth of the suggestion ofEberstaller and Cunningham, that the fronto-orbital sulcus of the Apes [and Lemurs] is the anterior limiting sulcus of the insula of the human brain.

In most Ungulates, in many Carnivores, and in the Bradypodidz and illyswzecophagu, a small oblique sulcus makes its appearance in the position exactly corresponding to that occupied by the fronto-orbital sulcus in the Primates : it is called the diagoiial sulcus (Icrueg), and is placed between the orbital and the snprasylvian sulci. It is highly improbable that a sixall oblique sulcus such as the diagonal (especially in the Ungulates and in Brctclypzcs) should develop in the analogous pobition in three different Orders so as to exactly reproduce the features of the fronto-orbital sulcus of the Lemurs and Apes n.ithout being homologous. The probability that “ diagonal,” “ fronto-orbital,” and 4‘ anterior limiting ” are merely different names for the corresponding furrows in different mammals is immeasurably greater than the reverse.

The evidence upon which Professor D. J. Cunningham chiefly relied to clinch the proof

- o€ the ideaatity of the fronto-orbital and anterior limiting sulci, was the distribution of the claustrum. This consists of the deeper part of the cortex which csmposes tlie island
- of ReiL. In the human brain the claustyan becomes separated from the rest of the cortex by a definite medullary layer in a region coextensive with the insula, i. e. so far forward as the aiihrior limiting sulcus. IDthe Chimpanzee [and the same holds also for the Orang and Gorilla] the claustrum extends as far forward as the fronto-orbital sulcus. I n the Cercspitliecidae and CebidiR it is unfortunately the fact that we cannot use sucli evidence with the same degree of definiteness, because the anterior extremity of the

Among Clark's conclusions it is stated that " the claustrum may include parts of ayeas other than the insula " (p. 91), i. e. it may extend beyond the situations of the Limiting sulci.

The most revolutionary and novel suggestion in the foregoing discussion is the homology of the suprasylvian sulcus of riiammals in general partly with the superior limiting SU~CUSand partly with the upper (or posterior) part of the Sylvian fissure of the Lemurs and Apes.

Such an interpretation of the morphology of the cerebral sulci has never bee11

suggested hitherto ; and yet I believe that, if the identity of these two sulci be denied, it is impossible to homologize the majority of the sulci in the brain of the Primates with those of other mammals. For if the furrow which is deepest, most stable, and most precocious in tlie Carnivora, Ungulata, and Edentata is not represented in the Primates, and, moreover, by that particrrlar sulcus which is also most constant and develops earliest, then it is utterly futile to seek for the hr?mologues of the other more variablc sulci.

The only writer (Ziehen) who has seriously attempted to hotnologize the Prosimian sulci with those of other mammals [or, more strictly, with those of the Carnivora], foiind representatives in the Primate brain for the lateral, coronal, ansate, crucial, presylvian, ectolateral, meclilsteral, intraorbital, and postsylvian [his "fissura suprasylvia posterior "3 of the Carnivora, and yet imagined that the most stable and deepest sulcus of thc: Carnivora-the suprasylvian sulcus *-was missing when so many unimportant sulci arc reproduced, Such a suggestion is utterly inconceivable ; and, even if we knew nothing of the instructive plienornena exhibited in Claif-on.tys,in Myrmecophugu,and in Doliehotis, the conclnsion must be forced upon us that either the suprasylvian sulcus is merged in the Sylvian complex, or it is impossible to institute exact couiparisoas between the sulci of the Primates and any other mamma!s.

It is quite unnecessary to point out tlie fallacy of the old teaching of Pansch aiid Xeynert, that tlic suprasylvian sulcus represents the intraparietal sulcus of the Primates.

Several writers have appreciated the obstruction in the way of a satisfactory interpretation of this ~egion;but I believe no one has hitherto suggested the solution which all impartial observers must admit to be true.

Thirty years uyo Gervais saw the difEculty, when he wrote :-'6 Lemurs never have more than two convolutions around the Sylvian fissure, whilst even the sivallest Carnivores have three " f ; but did no%explain which of the Carnivore furrows disguisctl its identity by rncrging in another sulcus.

Quite recently Roll recognized that the '' Sylvian fissure " of Leimi- is composed of' two separate sulei ; but he suggested the ectos?lvian, and not the suprasylvian, as the dorml element 5 .

Tn the interpretation of the morphology of sulci, one is not disposed to place any great reliance upon pbpsiological eviderice ; for, even supposing that the functions of the various regions mere known, it does not necessarily follow that homologous areas are liomodpamic in different Orders. Nevertheless it is of interest to note how fully the results of physiological researc’n support the view that the upper part of the Sylvia11 fissure represents the suprasylvian sulcus.

Thus Ferricr records * that stimulation of the dorsal lip of the suprasylvian sulcus in the Cat causes retraction of the angle of the mouth and movement of the eyes to tlic opposite side, whereas pricking or sudcien retraction of the opposite ear is tlie result of applying the electrodes to the lower lip of the suprasylvian sulcus. Stiinulatiolz of tlbe coi*respondiizg lips of the zrpper pccrt of the Sylciun Jssure in a monkey (Macacus) pyoducecl the suine results us t l d elicitecl by excituliun o f the lips o f the szcprccsyluicci~ sulczcs in the Cut (fig.335). The results obtained by Ferrier in the case of Lepzcs (p. 731.) are in full accord.

In Edinger’s suininary of the results of physiological experiment on the cerebral cortex lie represents two diagramst, borrowed from Gustav Mann, showing the lateral aspectof the brains of the Cat and Rabbit respectively : in these the suprasylvian sulcus is represented separating two areas labelled respectively “Oculomotnr ” [the dorsal] and “Auris ” lthe- l-entral]. Edinger inserts a diagram of the human brain alongside these, and labels the dorsal lip of tlie posterior end of the Sylvian fissure “Oculomot.” and the ventral lip “Buditus.”

###### Now, as it would be absurd to imqiiae that the results adopted by Ferrier, Scliafer,

Mann, and Edinger were in any way biassed by such a (to them) rank heresy as tlic belief in the identity of the Sylvian and suprasylvian sulci, the evidence which they unconsciously produce is of value as an indication that one who entertains such a belief cannot be accused of flagrantly disregarding physiological data. Noreover, this wholly unexpected support from the physiologists is the last link in a lony chain of irrefutable evidence (only a few links of which have been incorporated in these notes) that the upper (caudal] part of the Sylvian fissure of the Primates and the dorsal limiting sulcus of Reil (the <‘ opercular ” sulcus of Marchand) represent the suprasylvian sulcus of quadrupeds.

The stable suprasylvian su1cus, by its “ union ” witli that variable suprarhinal kin]<

which I have called ‘‘pseudosylvian sulcus,” gives a fixity to the latter which it did not possess before ;and the result is the Sylvian fissure, or, to be strictly accurate, part of tho posterior limb of the fissura Sylvii.

In the general review of the condition of the sulci in the Mammalia, it was noted that in the Rodent Doliehotis and in the Edentate Hymzecophup a peculiar union of the suprasylvian and pseudosylvian sulci occurs. In the light of the foregoing discussion, tbcce facts may he simply expressed by the statement that these two niammals have a Prosimian Sylvian fissure.

Carnivores, the student next examines the brain in the Arctoiciea, he will find that the whole of the first arcuate gyrus of Leuret has become submerged in a great cleft in the situation of the pseudosylvian sulcus, and that there is a pronounced tendency for thc anterior limh of the second arcuate gyrus to become submerged. This tendency, which is seen in most Bears, becoincs more marked in HeZes; and in most of the Seals thc whole of the anterior limb of the second arcuate gyms and the greater part of the suprasylvian sulcus hcome swept hito the pseudosylvian cleft. There is therefore in the drctoid Carnivores a tendency to the production of a condition which is analogour to, without however being altogether identical with, the state of affairs which has already become evolved in the Primates. It is of interest to note that when the suprasylvian sulcus is swept into the pseudosylvian sulcus in Otaricc, Odobcenus [‘r*icheclms], aud Phocct, it becomes separated from the postsylvian sulcus (to which it is united in most Carnivores). When the suprasylvio-pseudosylvian complex is formed in Xyrmecophaga, the postsylvian sulcus becomes disconnected ; but in the anomalous case (vide szipm) in which the suprasylvian sulcus is not joined to the pseudosylvian, the former sulcus was joined to the postsylvian.

These peculiar phenomena are of great interest in the interpretation of the Prosimian brain. It has frequently been noted in this memoir that in the Prosimie, and especially in the genus Lemzw, the mode of ending of the upper extremities of the suprasylviaii and pseudosylvian sulci is exceedingly variable, and indubitably points to a connection between these two furrows, which has only recently been broken. In the peculiarly interestin: brain of Clziromnys,the suprasylvian and the variable pseudosylvian sulci are widely separated, as in most quadrupeds; and, like the parallel case afforded by the aberrant brain of Myi*).lzecophaga,the suprasylvian sulcus then becomes joined (in 11204 cases) to the postsylvian sulcus.

The extreme variability of the postsylvian sulcus in Chiromys is chiefly the result of t,he peculiar inconstancy of tlie pseudosylvian su!cus ;for it will be obvious, from an examination of the brains of Procavia and the Unqulata, that the characters of the postsylvian (which is a morpliologicallg unstable sulcus) are determined chiefly by the nature of the pseudosylvian sulcus. The irregularity of the postsylvian sulcus may also be partly due to the fact that Chi;l*oiizp has obviously been subject to marke:d retrogressii e changes.

In most Lemurs the pstsylvian sulcus is a simple linear furrow, which obviously represents the parallel or superior temporal sulcus of the Apes and Man.

,~lthougll tlie pseudosylvian sulcus has a morphological importance and stnbility \yhich are immeasurably less than that of the suprasylvian sulcus, it is obvious that it is the former which is the chief determinant of the peculiar “Sylvian formation.” “}]at this is so is shown by a sludp of the Carnivora, in many of which (as, for instance, in many Canidz, the larger Pelid=, and Ilyznidtie) a condition so spuriously like that of the true Sylvian fissure is produced without any participation by the suprasylvian sulcus, that the mistake of regarding the Carnivore pseudosylvian sulcus and the Prilllate Svlvian fissure as homologues is not unnatural. The suprasylvian sulcus, however, adds stability to a region which in other mammals is notoriously variable and fickle. Tile

I shall discuss first of all tlie representation of these sulci in the general body of mamnials, and then follow tlie evoiution of the correspondin: furrows in the Apes.

If we follow the example of Zielien, and limit our ma,mii~alirtnhorizon to such specialized Carnivores as Cuais and Phocu, neglecting all the generalized Carnivores and the Ungulates, Eclentates, and Rodents, we might perhaps arrive at the same conclusion and regard thc sulcus I have called ‘‘lateral ” as the honiologne of the conjoint coronolateral furrow of many Carnivores. If, however, we study the developmental tendencies not only in one or two specialized f‘oi’ms, but in the whole l\iIainmalia (in such a iiianner as I hare ronghly sketched out already), the whole evidence will point to the conclusion that the sulci whicli I have called “lateral ’’ aiid ‘(coronal ” respectively are in reality the honiologues of the sulci of the same iiames in Vivemh, P~“o~uciia,and all marnmais. I n other words, tlie coronal sdcus, instead of being represented in the furrow which 1 have called “lateral,” as Ziehen would have us believe, really exists in the Lemurs as

the separate sulcus which I have called “coronal.” The latter sulcus (Ziehen’sc) is

It is seen j11

The prorean sulcus has a peculiar distribution in the mimmalian series.

both the Polyprotodont (Fhylacinus)and Diprotodont Marsupials (l\ilacropodiclze,PI~usco-

lon2ys, and extinct Y’hylucoleo), in -Mymecoplac(gu and Orycfei*opcsalone of thc Edentata, rtntl reaches the height of its development in the Carnivora, and especially in some of the &ctoidea, such as Gulo. If we seek for this sulcus in the more generalized Carnivora, and especially the Viverridz, no such separate element as the prorean sulcus will be found. In the Viverridce the coronal sulcus is prolonged forward so as to take the place, functionally, if not morphologically, of the prorean sulcus.

,4 similar phenomenon occurs in most Ungulates, and in the generalized Hyrax (Procncia) the coronal sulcus extends far forward on to the apex of the hemisphere. 3lucll liqtit is thrown upon this perplexing problem by a coniparisoii of the arrangemwt of these sulci in certain Rodents and Edentates with that found in other mammals.

I n the Carnivora the coronal is separated from tlie prorean sulcus by a mesial extension of the orbital (presylvian) SU~CUS. This prolongation is certainly a purely mechanical phenomenon ; i. e., the tension of the growing cortex becomes relieved hy the extension of a preexisting furrow, which in this case is the orbital. I n Oqcte,*opzcs, and sometimes in Myrmecophugu,the mesial prolongation of the orbital is united not only with the prorean but also with the anterior end of the coronal sulcus. In the brain of Doliehotis (Rodentia) the orbital sulcus is not prolonged beyond its morphological limits (if such a phrase is permissible), but the corono-prorean complex retains a form which is curiously reminiscent of the united coronary and prorean sulci in O~ycteropz~.This

11ould seem to indicate that the ‘‘coronal ” SU~CUSof the Viverridz, of the Unyulata, :and of the Lemurs ought properly to be regarded as prorean-coronal. For the constant development of the prorean SU~CUS in those brains (of such widely separated mammals :I,? the Marsupials, Edentates, and Carnivores) in which its union vith the coronal sulcus is prevented by a “meclianical ” extension of the orbital seems to indicate that the proyean sulcus possesses some definite individuality and morphological stability.

In the brain of Chi~omysthere is a peculiar modification of this region (which slion-s unmistakable signs of retrogressive change). In some cases there is a definite typical orbital sulcus and, in a more dorsal position, another sulcus whicli is oliviously the anterior part of a coronal (or prorean-coronal) sulcus which has liecome broken into two fragments, like that of l~)cticebus. In other cases tlie two sulci ti. e. the orbital and the anterior fragment of tlie proresn-coronal complex) become united. Oudemans calls this sulcus simply “prorean.” I think that Oudeiaaiis’s and Zichen’s use of tlie term “ prorean,” without being ahsolutely erroneous, fails to express the whole truth.

There can be no difficulty in interpreting the sulci lateralis and coronalis in terms which are applicable to the Apes, because these two sulci (in the form seen in the genus Lemzw) are exactly reproduced in some of the snialler Cebidz, such as Cdlith~i.?-.

The lateral sulcus becomes the intraparietal (or “ interparietal,” as tlie Germans call it) SU~CUS; and the coronal sulcus is identical with that 1-ariouslp termed ‘‘fi*oiztcclis pi*ir~cipciZis,~’“wetus,” “ ~ o ~ t i w l iamongs , ~ ~ many other designations. It includes the representatives of the two sulci called respectively “mwhs ” and ‘‘ai*cuatus” in the Apes. The latter certainly represents the inferior precentral sulcus of the human brain ; wliereas the homology of the former is the suliject of great divergence of opinion. IIerv4, Eberstaller, Waldeyer, Giacomini, and Dubois * consider that the sulcus rectus hecomes the sulcus frontalis medius of Eherstaller in the human brain ; mhercas Cunningham and Marchand incline to the old view of Gratiolet, that it represents the sulcus frontalis inferior. A s I have shown in the above notes that the fronto-orbital sulcus (which Waldeyer, Giacomini, and their school regard as the homolope of the inferior frontal sulcus) is certainly the anterior limiting sulcus of Reil, the evidence seems to point to the view of Cunninghain and Marchand as the true interpretation.

The postlateral sulcus is as 1-ariable in the Lemurs as its representative in tlle Carnivora and other maminals is unstable. Bcddard, Ziehen, and Flatau and Jacobsohr; call it parieto-occipital ; Chudzinski labels it “occipital,” a term which is apparently applied in the same way as “ parieto-occipital ” used by the other writers. Beddart1 further complicates matters by applying the term “ Simian ” [i.e. Affenspalte] to it in Kycticebus (Proc. Zool. SOC.1895, p. 1/44)

It is unnecessary to enter any further into the discussion of the later evolution of this sulcus in the Apes, for my views on this subject have been far more aptly expressecl

than I am capable of stating them, in Cunningham's monograph*. There still remains for discussion the most difficult problem in the interpretation of the Prosimian sulci, i. e. whether the central (Rolanclo's) sulcus is represented in any form in the Lemurs.

The central sulcus is easily recognizable in all the Simiiclz and Cercopithecidz. Sou can there be much uncertainty concerning its identity in the larger Cebiclz. If the smaller Cebidae be studied without comparison with the larger members of tho family. it may seem very doubtful whether the small central sulcus in such a form as Chrysotlwix (for example) might not really represent the ramm postcentralis of tho intraparietal SU~CUS. But an examination of t,hebrain in the whole Family shows that the postcentral sulcus first appears long after the central itself is fully formed. Sothat in those Cebidz in which only one sulcus is present in the appropriate place, it may he regarded as the central.

The simplest form of a well-defined central SU~CLLSmay be seen in the genus Pithecia

- as a simple transverse sulcus in the interval between the intraparietal and ''coronal" sulci.

I n the genus CuZZithrixthere is sometimes no trace of a central sulcus ; in other oases there is merely a shallow depression in front of the intraparietal sulcus, and the situation

It will lie seen, therefore, that there is little semblance of agreeincnt with recaulI to the central sulcus in the three most’ recent memoirs on the Prosimian brain.

There is no necessity to again revien- all tlie data afforded by a study of the genera

- J J w i u ~ ,h?~cticebus,I’wotlz’cticiis, YmjiitlLeczrs, and Chikomys. It is sufficient to rec:ill that a very pronounced tendcncy is exhibited in these varied forms for the sninll sulcnsf to fuse with tlie caudal extremity (e) of the coronal sulciIs, with the resulting formation of a great transverse sulcus, which can be nothin: clsc than the central sulcus of the Primates.

It does not come within the scope of this work to consider iii all its bearings tlie vexed question of the relationships of the Leinuroidea. It would, indeed, he presuniptuous to attempt such a difficult task, which has baffled many zoologists with the Iwst equipment for performing it. All that I am concerned with at present is the setting forth of such evidence as the brain affords, and the determination of its value as an index of the afGnity of its possessor to other mammals.

The greatest divergence of opinion exists aniong zoologists as to the relative closeness of the bonds which link the Lemurs and the Apes.

Many writers, such as Gervais and Milne-Edwards, have relcgatecl the former to an Order quite distinct and separate from the Apes ; and Flower virtually, though not nominally, subscribes to the same opinion, for he retains the Lemurs among the Yriim,tes chiefly in deference to “the traditional views of zoologists.” 011the other hand, LinnEus, Lesson, Huxley, Broca, and Mivart assign them a subordinate rank in the Primates. Nevertheless Mivart, who is generally quoted as a champion of this view, has stated ‘‘that the differences between the apes and lemurs are so many and so great, that it cannot be considered otherwise than in the hiqhest degree improbable that (on the evolutionary hypothesis) they took origin from any coninion root-form that was not equally tlie progenitor of other inaninialian Orders ”f. So that Nii-art also virtually places himself in the ranks of those who favour a separate ordinal rank for the 1,eniurs.

Dr. Porsyth Major’s careful studies of the skeleton and tretli of the recent L2miirb, and of an interesting series of extinct Lemuroids, within the past few years, liavc utterly stultified Flower’s statement that “as a more complete knowlodge of their organization lins been gradually attained, tlie interval which separates them structurally from the monkeys has become gradually more evident.” Forsyth BIajor’s conclusion is SO diametrically opposedto Flower’s teaching that, to quotehis own words, his “position ... . will have to be expressed in classification by giving LIP the two separate suborders of Primates, thus going one step further than Mivart ” $.

418 PROF. G. ELLTOT SMlTH ON THE

It is hardly necessary to insist on the value of cerebral characters as an index of the affinities of the animal, when it is recalled that the mammalian brain is perhaps the only organ which can be truly said to represent in itself the whole organism. For even though the brain is responsive to changes of habit in the animal, and innumerable other factors which in various mays influence the proportions of its various parts and modify its shape, yet, in its essential plan, it is perhaps the niost conservative organ in the whole body. Thus the same fundamental plan of the brain is found throughout the Eutheria, and, with the exception of the striking changes in the constitution of the cerebral comniissures, it is common also to all the Metatheria. In the foregoing notes I have attempted to show that underlying the exceedingly variable and apparently haphazard disposition of the sulci of the cerebral hemisphere-which, being the most recently-modified, are consequently the most changeable regions of the brain-there is a fundamental group of homologous furrows, which are the common property of tlie whole Meta- and Eutheria rather than the exclusive distinction of one Order.

The fact of having thus discarded as obsolete and unjustifiable the belief that each Order of mammals evolves for itself a number of furrows which are incapable of being homologized with those of other Orders, may seem to have broken down the barriers which separate the Lemurs from other mammals (for wen those who liave most inagnified the differences between Apes and Lemurs liave been compelled to adopt from t!ie higher Primates their nomenclature for the Prosimian sulci). But this is by no means so. For, having demonstrated the identity of the various elements vliich may unite in various manners to form the most heterogeneous series of patterns in the different Orders, we are the better able to appreciate the Ordinal value of these varied groupings of the series of sulci, the morphological values of wiiich we know, than we should if a new set of features of unknown importance were evolved in cwh Order.

It surely does not lessen the great systematic importance of the fact that the calcarine and suprasylvian sulci behave in a very peculiar and identical nianner in tlie Lemurs ;ind Apes, to know that the direct homologues of these furrows are disposed in a btrikingly different mann5r in the various other mammalian Orders. On the contrary, the fact that the different patterns are composed of such stable elements enhances tilth value of the plan of the sulci as an index of relationship.

The peculiar constancy of the arrangement of the calcarine, retroealcarine, and paracalcarine sulci is alone sufficient to indicate the close bonds of affinity which uilite a11 the Lemurs, and even such different members as Tumius and cYhii*owys,and at the same time separate them from all other mammals.

The confluence of the calcarine and retrocalcarine sulci is peculiarly distinctive of t11t. Primates, and in this respect the Lemurs conform to the Ordinal type. In many of the Sew-World Apes the paracalcarine sulcus is much less perfectly developed than it is ill tlie Lemurs ; aud in this respect the latter closely resemble the highest Apes, in whicll the paracalcarine sulcus forms the ventral element in that peculiar combination of ti\-() sulci which is commonly distinguished as " parieto-occipital." In making this statement I am not unmindful of the probability that this type of pal-acalcarine SU~CUSinay be merely its most primitive form.

[Figure 101]

JIORPHOLOGP OF THE BRAIN 1 3 THE MAJSMALIA. 419

The arrangement and behaviour of the sulci on the outer aspect of the hemisphere closely follow those of the Ape, and especially of the lowlier Cebidae. There is the same peculiar form of suprasylvian sulcus merged in the Sylvian fissure in both Families. The lateral sulcus is closely similar, as in fact it is in all mammals, but itl shows a peculiar tendency, which is exhibited in Nycticebus in the Lemurs aud in

C'lirysothrix, Nyctipitliecus, and Mycetes in the Cebidae, to become concurrent with t h r ~

suprasylvian (Sylvio-intraparietal union), which is a striking instmce of the wide separation of the Primates from all other mammals (in most of which the first stage of this process, i. e. the pushing, as it were, of the suprasylvian sulcus on to the pseudosylvian sulcus, has not begun, so that the further stage of pushing the lateral sulcusinto the Sylbian complex is not even possible); at tlie same time it is a further testimony to the affinity of the Lemurs and the Cebidae.

So far as t!ie other sulci-the corcnal, the orbital, the diagonal (fronto-orbital), the central rudiment, the postsylvian, and the postlateral-are concerned, there is the closest identity between all of these in the Lemurs and such lowly Cebidae as Cctllithris-, the importance of which is again enhanced by the contrast afforded by the behaviour of their respective representatives in other mammals.

There is no sulcus, perhaps, more distinctive of the Primates than the normallyconstituted central (Rolando's) sulcus. Such a sulcus (as a unit) is not found in any other Order of mammals; but there is a widespread tendency in the Lemurs-in Perodicticus of the Lorisinae, in certain cases in Propitlzeczcs, and in Cliiyornys-to the development of this characteristic sulcus.

The fronto-orbital sulcus, which, like the paracalcarine, is absent in many New-World Apes, is often as fully deleloped in the Lemurs (Aemw,Propithems, and lizclris)as it is in the larger Cercopithecidae. And even if this furrow (as in the analogous case of the paracalcarine) is represented among the other mammals (in the diagonal sulcus), j t it still important that a furrow which is not present in many New-World Apes sboultl occasionally develop in the Lemurs in the same situation and have the same form as the fronto-orbital sulcus in the larger Apes.

The degree of affinity of one mammal to another is a purely relative matter, which it is difficult to express adequately. But if we take all the features of neopallial sulci into consideration, it can confidently be stated that, judged by the standard of the other mammalian subdivisions, the differences between the brain in the Lemurs and such Primates as the CebidE are certainly not worthy of being granted either Ordinal or even Subordinal importance.

The differencesbetween the brain in the Eclentate Families RlyrmecopliagidE, Dasypoclidae, and Bradypodidae (which we recognize to be united by bonds of affinity) are considerably greater than those which divide the Lemurs and Cebidz. Or, to adopt a more certain or, at least, more generally recognized scale of relationships, there is tl much closei* reseniblance between the brain in the Lemurs and Apes than there is between those of the Bluroid and Arctoid Carnivores.

So far as the neopalliai furrows are concerned, the Lemurs might be regarded as inseparable from the Cebidae, if it were not for the fact that the other parts of the br5117 exhibit very definite distinctive features.

In all of these Carnivora the proportion of the brain-weight to the body-wcight is less (and in many cases very considerably so) than it is in Lemurs with brains of a corresponding size. And in other mammalian Orders the disproportion in the sizc of tlie body per unit of brain-weight is even more pronounced. The Lemurs all have relatively large hrains in comparison n-itli other niarnmals, but are rnnch more poorly endowed in this respect than tlie Apes.

The data are too scanty to enable us to decide whether this difference in tlie braiiiTveiglits of Apes and Lemurs is due nierely to the failure of tlie latter to keep pace in hrain-growth with tlie former, or is the result of an actual reduction in size.

The most noteworthy distinction between the brain of the LWXLLUSand tlie ,4p is the i~latii-esize of the olfactory regions of tlie brain.

The brain of tlie Apes is distinctly microsmatic. The olfactory bull, is very sinall, and is borne on a long attenuated peduncle ; the anterior part of the pyriforiii lobe is so reduced as to be recognizable with difficulty, and tlie posterior part is so sniall that it is almost alwags confined to the ventro-mesial aspect, and rarely extends on to the lateral aspect of tlie hemisphere ; and the hippocampus is relatively very siiiall.

I n mammals tlie size of the olfactory parts of tlie brain is subject to great

.\-ariatiom.

Thus in all aquatic mammals they become greatly reduced in size (e.8. in Oi*iLith-

9*Iu~1ic?~zts,the Pinnipedia, the Sirenia, the Cetacea), or even entirely absorbed (in tlie Odontoceti). I n niaminals which lead an arboreal life they are relacively smaller than in those nhich lead a terrestrial existence, e. 8.the rhinencephalon is relatively much

[Figure 104]

422 PROF. G.ELLIOT SILITH OX THE

smallep in the arboreal Cyclotzcrus than in the terrestrial ilIyiwzecophcrga, two Edentates of the same family; but it is the peculiarity of the Apes that they have become extremely microsmatic for no apparent reason, the diminution of their olfactory organs being much more pronounced than it is in any other arboreal animal. This phenomenon is undoubtedly to be mainly attributed to the increasing efficacy of the neopallium as an “organ of mind,” and its consequent usurpation of the 4 e of guiding the creature, which elsewhere among mammals is fulfilled chiefly by the sense of smell.

It is a very significant fact that the Lemur which exhibits evidence of being the most primitive- Tamizcs-possesses the most extensive occipital prolongation, and is more microsmatic than any other of the Prosimiz. Thus the olfactory bulb of Tarsius is only half as large as that of Jfic~ocehs;and yet the former is appreciably greater than that of iKidas.

The importance which has been attached to the amount of cerebellum covered by the cerebral hemisphere as an index of the degree of brain-development is altogether disproportionate to its true significance. By this I do not mean to imply that the degree of caudal prolongation of the hemisphere is not a very iinportant indication of the size of the cerebral hemisphere, but it must not be forgotten that it is merely one of several indications of the degree of cerebral expansion. The failure of the cerebral hemispheres to completely overlap the cerebellum is an obvious point of distinction between tho brain of the Lemurs and most Apes. In the Lemurs the extent of the backward prolongation of the hemisphere varies considerably. In Nycticehus, for example, there is only a very narrow strip of cerebellum uncovered. In the Galaginae and in Tnmizrs . the extent of the caudal prolongation of the hemisphere is perhaps greater than in any other Lemur; i. e., if its dimensions are estimated in relation to the position of the corpus callosiun and by the size of the calcar, rather than by the situation of the caudal pole of the hemisphere with relation to the cerebellum. The relative greatness of the occipital extension of the hemisphere in these particular forms is all the more significant, because Taal-sius and GuZago are tlie Lemurs which have probably become least changed (so far as the brain is concerned) from the early Prosimian type. It is hence probable that the immediate ancestors of the Lemurs possessed brains of a more pithecoid form. There are many other suggeative facts which point in the same direction.

In most mammals tlie calcarine and intercalary (?paracalcarine) sulci beconic continuous (pbabiy for purely mechanical reasons), and it is hardly conceivable that the small caudal extension of the hemisphere in the recent Lemurs is sufficient to so completely dissociate the calcarine from the intercalary sulcus, and to lead to its confluence with the retrocalcarine sulcus, or in fact to supply the mechanical factors which call the latter sulcus into being. It is difficult to conceive of the present relations of the three sulci of the calcarine group having been produced except in a brain with a much more pronounced extension of the hemisphere than at present exists.

It has been shown by Prof. A. Milne-Edwards, and emphasized by Dr. Forsyt,hI\lajor*,

that in the young P~opitheczisEdu;u7dsi the caudal extension of the cerebral hemisphere over the cerebellum is distixtly greater than it is in the adult. From an esamination of the cranium of a youny (13 days old) Pg*opit?aecuscomnc~tus,which Dr. Porsgth Major lent me, I have been able to establish this also for another species of a later age. However, in the brain of' a young (15 days old) Lenzui*fzclzzcs, which Captain Stanley Flower gave me, the cerebellum was exposed to as great an extent as in the adnlt. In clifferent specimens of adult Lemurs, even of tlie sarne species, I hum fotutid considerable variety as regards the amount of cerehelluiii uncovered.

If we consider the inarked disproportion in tlie size of the brain in the Lemurs and the Apes respectively, the possibility presents itself that the former may at oce tinio have been much larger than it is now. If sucli u7ere the case, it is certain that thp process of reduction in size would be cliieflj- expressed in tt curtailment of the caudal (occipital) prolongation.

A study of the peculiar series of 1-ariutions in the Indrisinz, Chii*omys,and to a Iess extent in all other Lemurs, clearly shows that tlie hypothesis of a rctrogressive changc can alone explain this extreme variability.

It may be urged that it is not the mere retraction, so to speak, of the caudal pole of the hemisphere which distinguishes the occipital regioii of the Lciii~irsfrom that of the Apes, but also the absence of the posterior cornu in the former. This is a, feature the importance of which has been grosdy exaggerated. It is rnerely one of the expression$ of a large neopallium, and the question of the presence or absence of the calcar and calcarine sulcus is not in any way involved in the issue. In any large mammal (large mammals being provided with actually, though not relatively, more extensive neopallia thaii the small) the increase in size of the neopallium may lead to the formation of a posterior cornu. We see this in the Carnivora, Ungulata, and Cetacea, as well as in tile Primates. The Primates, being preemineiit in neopallial greatness, naturally preseiit in most cases a distinct posterior coriiu.

The direct result of the "retraction " of the occipital extension of the hemisphere

.cr-ouldbe the obliteration of the posterior cornu.

Tile brain of Tc(7*sius,which in most respects is so eminently Propithecoid, is distinguishcd from other Lemurs by the possession of a distinct posterior cornu indistinguishable from that of the smallest New-World Apes.

m-hcn it is rccallecl that the Eocene Lemuroids possessed brains no smaller in size than those of the recent forms (Cope), it 11 ould indeed be surprising if the brain shotnld 1laT-ereixained stationary in size through all the succeeding ages.

SOfar as the brain is concerned, the Galaginae are (with the exception of Tctrsius) tlic lllost generalized of all the Lemurs. And, in view of the foregoing suggestions, it is not TVithout significance that they also possess the most pronouncccl cccipital prolongation.

~ j l cerebrale features of tlie meinbers of the genera L e m w and Bcymlemur are not ftlr removed froni those of Gnlugo. The peculiar variability in the extent of cerebellum coyered by the cerebrum is suggestive of a "reti-action" of the latter.

Far rnore specialized than these genera are the Indrisinz, Lcpiclolen~z~vbeing in most respects intermediate between the latter aiid Len2.u~. In different modes the Indrisinc

type of brain is peculiarly specialized in tlic genus C?zii*onzys and the extinct Lemuroid ,lIegci Incluyis.

It is impossible, with any degree of accuracy, to place the peculiarly pitliecoid form of brain which Globilenziw prescilts. So far as its clmracters can he determined, it rescinbles the type of the genus L~nzzc~and the Galagin=.

The Lorisin2 again prescnt peculiar cerebral features. In many respects the brain is peculiarly simple, after the mine niaimer as that of the Galaginzc. I n Perodicticzcs, and to a less extent in Nycticcbzcs, there is a peculiar teiidency toward the formation of the typically Primate ccntral sulcus.

Tui-sius is obviowly related, so far as the evidence of the brain is concerned, to the Galaginae. This reseniblaucc cannot bc disguised even by the peculiar distortion of the brain in the former. It is in every respect distinctly Prosimian. But it also exhibits two interesting series of features. So far as the corpus callosum, the hippocainpnl formation, and the cerebellum are concerned, the brain of Tarsius conforms to the simplest Eutherian type, such as we find in the more generalized Insectivorafor example, Binuceus. Pet its reltltivc microsmatism (in comparison with other Lemurs), the proportion of its hemisphere which is placed on t’he caudal side of the corpus callosum, and the presence of a definite posterior cornu of the lateral ventricle, all indicate a nearer approach to thc Sjniiaii condition than occurs in the brain of any other Lemur. Tcwsius possesscs at once tlie most generalized and the most pithecoicl brain of all the Leniuroiclcs.

I n this connection it is intcresting to quote some remarks concerning A72~6~tOWZOrph~~s

which were made seventeen years ago by E.D. Cope :-“ The brain and its heniisphercs are not at all smaller than those of the Tarsius, or of tlie typical leniurs of the present period. This is important in view of the very sinall brains of the flesh-eating and unqulate Mammalia of thc Eoceiic period as yet knonii. I n coiiclusion, there is no doubt but that the genus Ancptonaorplms is the rnmt Simian lemur yet discovered, and probably represents the family from which the anthropoid monkeys and men were derived”*.

Zittel also has remarked that the Eocene Tkinuroids (AcZctpis,JIic~och~i~us)of Europe ‘‘ combine features of the existing Lemurs and true Apes ”.f..

It is not easy to draw any certain conclusions froxi the evidence of the brain as to the relations of the Primates to other mammals.

The larger Carnivores show a tcndency toward the development of a Sylvian coniplex like that of the Primates. There is a singular absence of any such tendency in the Ungulata. Like the latter, however, the Primates always retain a coronal sulcus, which in the main is iiearer the liorizoiital than the vertical direction. It is a peculiar fact chat the separation of the calcarine and intercalary sulci, which is so marked a feature of the Primates, should occur (practically) nowhere else in the Mammalia, except in the three Edentate families Myrmecophagidq Bradypodide, and Manidae. I n the case of

thc Antcaters tlicrc is another peculiar Primate feature : the suprasylvian and pseuclosylvian sulci blend to form a Sylvian fissure. It is quite impossible to say how ninch importance is to bc attached to such phenomena.

A€ter seeking for some explanation for all the apparciitly conflicting feclturcs of tlie Prosimian brain, the following tentative working hypothesis as to the aiicestry of the Lemurs shaped itself in my mind, and I insert it here merely as a slender bond connecting certain facts scattered through these notes.

The Iwain of the Primates was derived from some Inscctivore-like type, the cerebral hemispheres of which attained a precocious development and, as one of the expressions of their grcatncss, bulged baclimard over the ccrebeilum. In consequence of this great extension of thc "pliysical organ of the associative nicmorp of visuitl, auclitory, and tactile sensations," the sensc of smell lost the predominance which it exercised in the primitive mammal (and in all the Orders of recent manimals), and the olfactory parts of the brain rapidly dwindled. This early Primate developed its distinctive type of calcarine sulcus and " Sylvian fissure," thc latcrai, coronal, and orbital sulci, a n d the characteristic central sulcus.

In the keen struggle for existcnce, the Lemurs ceased to keep pace with the other Primates so far as the increase in tlie size of the brain is concerned. They becamc more specialized, aiid their brain prol~ablyshrunk, thus leading to a retraction of the occipital pole of the hciiiisplieres.

With the diminution of the size of tlie neopallium the sense of smell comes to play a more important palat, and a secondary re-enlargement of the olfactory regions OCCLIPS. The blotting-out of the rhinal fissure may be an indication of this phenomenon.

This somewhat crude and tentative scheme is put forward to give expression to the view that the Lemurs are highly specialized Primates which share most of their brain-features in common with those of the Apes, and that some at least of the apparently primitive characters may be due to a secondary retrogressive modification of a more highly-developed type of brain.

the cerebellum of iiiaiiy inamnials in the current terminology ; and that even in the cme of those which could bc so descrihecl, the account woulcl be altogetlier unnatural aid hopelessly cunibrous. The chief reason for this is that the cerebellar fissure (fissum horizontalis map a ) , which is regarded in Human A4natoniy of such fundamentill importance as to be invariably taken as the starting-point in the primary subdivision, is a most insignificant cleft of quite secondary importance in most maninials, and is even abseiit altogcther in others.

It therefore becamo necessary to seek some more stable line of demarcation between the 1-ariouscerebellar regions. Accordingly I proposed a tentative scheme of subdirision lnsed upon the recognition of the fissure called c c preclival ” as the primary iiatural line of separation of the mesial parts of the organ. My reason for adopting this fissure was the fact that it is present in all niarnnials, m-ithout exception, and is, moreover, the deepest fissure crossing the mesial plane. Ample confirmation of the justice and iiaturalness of this selection was afforded by the fact that two other investigators liad independently come to the same conclusion-Stroud, as the result of a study of the development of the organ in the Cat and in Man, and Kuithan from embryological btudies on the Sheep.

It mill be seen that, in spitc of its smaller size, tlie cerebclluiii of the Iiisectivore is slightly richer in furrov-s than the Marsupial, and that, in order to find in the former Order a cerebellar pattern of the same degree of richness as Pel-cmeles exhibits, we must select some small form such as illctci-oscelidesor Oiyxoiyctes.

It will be seen that the pattern found in Tcwsizi8 is much richer in fissures than that of either tlie Insectirora or the JIarsnpialia. And, if it be objected that this contrast iiiay be due to tlic smaller size of the organ in Ecriizciceus, the suggestion can be a t once refuted hp showing that in the larger Insectivorc Gyiiz~zu~~c~,in Galeoyiilzecus, in many Iioclents like Lepus, and in the represeiitatives of other Orders of a corresponding size, tlierc is a greatcr poi-erty of fissures tlinn there is in 2%i*sizis. The mesial part of the cerebelluni is divided into three regions by means of tlie deep fissara priiiia,jf., and by the sl1allon7er fissura secunda,fs. (fig. 64). The region in front of the fissura prima (which is homologous -with t,hc so-called "preclival " fissure) may be called the anterior lobe, that between the fissura prima and the fiss. aesunda the central, or, better, middle lobe ; tlie third region beiiig the posterior lob& Tlac anterior and middle lobes may be quite simple undivided folia, as in Notoryctes, but the posterior lobe is always subdivided iiito two lobules, which correspond to the parts cdled ''nodulus " and ('uvula " respectively in I-Iurnaia Anatomy.

A coiiiparisoii of a series of marrmialiaii ccrcbella clearly show that the posterior lobe does not increase in size to so mwlied a dcgree as the anterior and especially tbe middle lobe do ; and, coiiversely, tlie ultiniate aubdivision of this lobe is cvident at a inucli earlier phase in the pliylogenetic history than that of the other two lobes.

The most noteworthy feature of the mesial section of the cerebellum in Tcwsizcs is the pluiiipiiess and relative greatness (i. e. in coinparison with that of other sinall mammals) of the middle lobe of the cerebellum.

"lie lon-ermost part of this lobe is much more deeply cut off from the rest than it is in Erii~acezcsand Peiwnzeles. The simple foliuin so formed represents the '(pyramis " of Human A4natomy. It is the dorsal lip of the fissura secunda, and the rest of the

[Figure 111]

MOKPHOLOGT OF THE RRAIS IN TIIE JIAJlJIALIA. 429

middle lobe is much iiiore deeply incised, i. e. has a much more extensive surface than that of all other mammals of a corresponding size.

Wherefore the cerebellum of Tu~siusexhibits definite s i p of superiority over that of other mammals when it is studied in mesial sectioii.

The resemblance of the surface of the cerebellum iii Ta7*sz’t?csto that of other sivall manimals is much closer than one niight expect from a study of the mesial section.

The two lobules (nodule and uvula) of the posterior lobe extend outward for a short distance only and then seem-to the naked eye-to stop abruptly. If,however, a series OF sections be examined microscopically, this lobe will be found to exlend outwards as an exceedingly attenuated band of cortex which suddenly expands upon the lateral aspect of tlie iniddle pcduncle of the cerebellum to form a short plui~pbilobulated mass-the flocculus.

The pyramid (which is the ventro-caudal lobule of the middle lobe) is prolonged outward into a broad band 011 the caudal aspect of tlie cerebellum. It arches outward and downward parallel to the margin of the uvula, so that it reaches the lower margin of the cerebelluin. It then passes forward and expands, just above the floccuLus, into a narrow, widely-projecting rod of cortex-the paraflocculus.

This paraflocculus is enclosed in a bony capsule formed by the petrous temporal. It is that part of the floccular lobe which is commonly called the “flocculus,” but it is not the flocculus of Human Anatomy.

Tlie cortex on the upper side of the paraflocculus is divided into a series of five or six bands By means of shallow notches. Ttie under surface is smooth. The distal extremity is bifid.

It is the distinctness of the broad cortical band joining the pyramid to the paraflocculus which specially emphasizes the resemblance of the cerebellum in Tarsius and that found in the generalized mammals. In most Primates this simple relationship becomes so completely disguised as to be quite unrecognizable.

For reasons which will appear later, it is desirable to have some term with which to distinguish the band of cortex linking thc pyramid to the paraflocculus. I shall therefore call it the “ copula pyramidis.” Exactly how much of the uniform lnnd (in Twsi2~s)is pyramid and how niuch is copula, will be appreciated later when the different fate of the two parts will be stndied.

Ttie copula pyramidis forms the lowermost lobule of the lateral part of the middle lobe. The rest of the latter is subdivided into three parts, which I distinguished by the

letters A, B, and C in the Edentate cerebellum (op. cit.).

- The area A is a simple band which forms the posterior lip of the fissura prima and extends outwards and forwards (on tlie anterior or cerebral surface) as far as the paraflocculus.
- The area B is a little rounded knob which forms the most laterally-projecting part of the so-called “lateral lobe ” of the cerebellum. It is subdivided into three folia by means of two deep fissures. The area B is wedged in between the lateral extremities a,f the areas A and C, snd is connected to the mesial part of the middle lobe only by a very narrow stalk, the liinitiiig furrows or”which we always shallow and sometimes absent.
