Thearea C is a little bifoliate nodule placed upon the caudal surface between tlie awn 13 anci the copula pyramidis which may be regarded as an area D. Gnlike tlie area R, C is frcely connected with the mesial parts by a broad band.

The fissures which indent the mesial part, so-called ‘(vermis,” of the midcllc lobe do not esteiid into the lateral regions. The limiting fissures of the arcns A, B, C, and D arise laterally and do not extcnd on to the mesial area in lkwsizis.

It is broadcst in the middle linc and gradually tapers toward the region of the entrance of the niidtlle peduncle into the cerebellum. Its surface is incised by two deep and three shallow tissurcs, the former of which extend as far as thc middle peduncle.

The anterior lobe differs in appearance from the other two lobes.

I n tlic brain of LCIIZU~=,the pattern exhibited in a mesial sagittal section of tlw ccrebelluin is mucli inore complicated than that of Tumizc.~,but the plan is csscntiall~+ the same (fig.65). It so closely resembles that of Ovycterop.9 which I htLve ctescribed in niy memoir on the brain in tlie Edentata (op. cit. fig. 29, p. 362), that it is unneeesssry to repeat the account. In the accompanying figure I have inserted letters to represent fissures homologous with those similarly distinguished in Ovycte~opzrs.

The general plan of the cerebellum closely conforms to the type found in such mamnials as tile Dog, thc Anteater, alid the smaller Apes.

Tlie floccinlar lobe is divided, as in all manimals, into a ventral part-the flocculusand a dorsal part-the paraflocculus. The flocculus consists of a little wedge-shaped inass closely applied to the lateral aspect of the pons Varolii immediately in front of the tuberculum acusticuin and external to the trigeininal nerve. Its broad anterior aspect is subdivided into folia by five or six horizontal fissures. The posterior extremity of the wedge-shaped mass is connected by a long white band with the nodulus.

The paraflocculus consists of an irregular mass of f o l k placed on the dorso-lateral aspect of tlie flocculus, so that in a lateral yiew of the brain the latter is almost coinplefcly hidden by it. From its iowerinost part (i.e. the region nearest the flocculus) a narrow stalk emerges and swells out into a plump projecting mass of folia arranged in a feather-like pattern. This projecting part of the paraflocculus is often called the

It presents a similar form in all Apes except the Simiidte. In €€yZobcrIes, however, tlie typical Primate forni of floccular lobe is fully developed.

petrosal lobule ” and more coinmonly simply “ flocculus.”

The nearest approach to this type of floccular lobe among other mammals is exhibited in the Ungulata, of which the accompanying drawing of the lateral aspect of the Gazelle’s cerebellum is typical (fig. 66).

In T~irsii(sthe paraflocculus Bas been described as being directly linked by a simple cortical band to the pyramid. In Lemw a vastly diflerent state of affairs obtains. Starting from thc stalk oi tlie so-called petrosal lobule, a series of folia (aud not a continuous undivided band) forins a worm-like coil which curves foru ard, then upmrartl and horizontally backward (compare the Gazelle’s cerebellum, fig. 66). The series of disconnected folia then proceeds baekw-aid below the area B to the lower end of the area C, and it becomes impossible to accurately separate the series of folia belonging to the parafloccular (or perhaps it would be more accurate to saj- (‘copular ”) group

[Figure 113]

MORPHOLOGY OF TXJ3 BRAIN IN THE AlAAI,llALIA. 4.31

from those of the area C. The connection of the copula pyramidis with the pyramid becomes either entirely destroyed or reduced to exceedingly insignificant proportions. From comparative studies of the adult cerebellum it is quite impossilJc to decide the fate of the copula pyramidis in the larger cerebella. In many brains it seems to dwindle or completely disappear. In others again, especially Si'imia scttyms, it seems to undergo enormous expansion and form the greater part, if not the whole, of the body which is called tonsilla (amygdala) in Human Anatomy. Thi3 is a problem which can only be certainly settled by a study of its developmental history in a series of mammals.

The relations of these parts are considerably disturbcd in the genera Xiinia, A~ztJwopo-

pithecus, and Bomo by the extreme dwindling of the paraflocculus and the consequent disappearance of its ''petrosal " part. The parafloccular remains become the flocculi secundarii of Henle.

Of the various subdivisions of the niiddle lobe of the cerebellum in Lemzw, the area ,4 is subject to perhaps the least change. It becomes furrowed by numerous new fissures, but they are approximately parallel to the fissura prima, and hence do not greatly disturb the pattern. The area A represents the lobus lunatus posterior, and is mesially continuous with the region between the fissura prima and the iissura b, which is called the clivus monticuli in Human Anatomy (fig. 6).

- The area B is a very plump rounded mass which forms the lateral pole of the cerebellum (fig.65). It consists of twelve vertical folia radiating in a feather-likc pattern from a very narrow vertically-placed leaf-like stalk, tlic niesial part of which is deeply buried between the areas A and C. This stalk docs not reach the niesial plane, for it becomes fused to the posterior aspect of the area A. The area 13 is the lobus posterosuperior.
- The area C is a plump vertical worm-like band wedged in between thc area 13 and the mesial payts of the middle and posterior lobes on the caudal surface of the cerebellum. Its connection vith the suprapyramidal part of the "verniis "is constricted and depressed in a vertical furrow. In the Apes this vertical band rapidly increases in breadth, and the direction of its folia becomes o5lique.

There is no separate folium cacuminis.

The general features of the anterior lobe are rernarliably constant in ali Xammals. The differences consist chiefly of an increase in the number of fissures in proportion to the size of the cerebellum.

In all other Prosimiae the structure of the cerebellum does not vary to any marked extent from that of Lemur. In the smaller niembers the fissures become much fen-cr, and in Jlicmrebus we find an organ which differs very slightly froni that of Tumizcs. In Cl~ii-oinysthe primitive connection bctircen tbc p'yimnid and the paraflocculus, whicli has been seen to become obscured in Lcmi~,is ietainccl in a distinct form.
